\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The functor
\pi_{0}
just maps a simplicial ring to its
0
-th homotopy ring,
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})^{\rm ab\,}
is the
abelianization
functor defined by

A\mapsto\frac{A}{(xy-yx\ |\ x,y\in A)}

and
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})^{{\mathbb{L}}{\rm ab\,}}
is the derived functor of the abelianization functor. We observe that this diagram of functors is commutative up to natural isomorphism. Also, the fact that
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})^{\rm disc\,}
is fully faithful can be checked easily by noticing that the counit map of the adjunction
\pi^{0}(A^{\rm disc\,})\to A
is a natural isomorphism.

The categories
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
have all limits and colimits. We briefly describe them. In both
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
products are given by the Cartesian product of the underlying set with coordinatewise operations. Similarly, pullbacks are given by the pullbacks of the underlying sets equipped with the natural ring operations induced by the inclusion into the Cartesian product. On the other hand, coproducts and pushouts in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are very different from those in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. We first describe the former case. The pushout of
A\to B
and
A\to C
in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is given by the ring

B\otimes_{A}C

and the coproduct of any small family
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is given by

\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}=\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in I^{n}}A_{i_{1}}\otimes_{R}\cdots\otimes_{R}A_{i_{n}}

where the colimit is taken over the family of all finite subsets of
I
. The pushout of two objects in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is given by the
free product
of algebras. For two morphisms
A\to B
and
A\to C
in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
this is defined by

(3.1)

B\ast_{A}C=\frac{T_{\otimes_{A}}(B\oplus C)}{({b\otimes_{A}b^{\prime}-bb^{\prime},c\otimes_{A}c^{\prime}-cc^{\prime},1_{B}-1_{C}\ |\ 1_{B},b,b^{\prime}\in B,1_{C},c,c^{\prime}\in C})}

where
T_{\otimes_{A}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
denotes the tensor algebra construction over
A
. The coproduct of any small family
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is given by

\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{i\in I}A_{i}=\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in I^{n}}A_{i_{1}}\ast_{R}\cdots\ast_{R}A_{i_{n}}

where again the colimit is indexed by all finite subsets of
I
.
We thus notice that the inclusion
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
does not commute with colimits. But the fact that
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})^{\rm ab\,}
is left adjoint immediately implies that

\left(\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)^{\rm ab\,}=\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}^{\rm ab\,},\ \ (B\ast_{A}C)^{\rm ab\,}=B^{\rm ab\,}\otimes_{A^{\rm ab\,}}C^{\rm ab\,}.

We recall that a colimit
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
in a category
{\mathbf{C}}
is said to be
filtered
if the indexing set
I
is a directed poset.
Although the functor
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
does not in general commute with colimits, it has the following (well-known) property.

Proposition 3.1
.

The inclusion
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
commutes with filtered colimits.

Proof.

Consider
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}A_{i}\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and let us denote
\iota:{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
the inclusion functor. Then, we need to show that the canonical map

\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})\to\iota(\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}A_{i})

is an isomorphism. Notice that since
\iota
is the right adjoint to the abelianization functor we have that

\left(\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})\right)^{\rm ab\,}\cong\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})^{\rm ab\,}\cong\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}A_{i}

because
\iota
is fully faithful. It is thus enough to check that
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})
is commutative. Let us consider
x,y\in\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})
. Since the colimit is filtered and the forgetful functor
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Sets}}
commutes with filtered colimits, there exists two elements
\widetilde{x}\in\iota(A_{i_{x}})
and
\widetilde{y}\in\iota(A_{i_{y}})
such that
\alpha_{i_{x}}(\widetilde{x})=x
and
\alpha_{i_{y}}(\widetilde{y})=y
, where
\alpha_{x}:\iota(A_{i_{x}})\to\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})
and
\alpha_{y}:\iota(A_{i_{y}})\to\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})
are the canonical maps. The fact that
I
is directed implies that there exists
k\in I
such that
i_{x}\leq k
and
i_{y}\leq k
. Thus, since
A_{k}
is commutative we have

(3.2)

x\cdot y=\alpha_{k}(\phi_{i_{x}}(\widetilde{x}))\cdot\alpha_{k}(\phi_{i_{y}}(\widetilde{y}))=\alpha_{k}(\phi_{i_{x}}(\widetilde{x})\cdot\phi_{i_{y}}(\widetilde{y}))\\
=\alpha_{k}(\phi_{i_{y}}(\widetilde{y})\cdot\phi_{i_{x}}(\widetilde{x}))=\alpha_{k}(\phi_{i_{y}}(\widetilde{y}))\cdot\alpha_{k}(\phi_{i_{x}}(\widetilde{x}))=y\cdot x\,,

where
\phi_{i_{x}}:A_{i_{x}}\to A_{k}
and
\phi_{i_{y}}:A_{i_{y}}\to A_{k}
are morphisms of the filtered system. Therefore
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}\iota(A_{i})
is commutative, and this completes the proof.
∎

3.1.1
Finitely presented algebras

Before moving on to the homotopical setting, let us recall some fundamental differences in the notion of finite presentation between the commutative and noncommutative settings. For the sake of clarity, we shall discuss objects and morphisms separately. Let us start with the former.
Let
R
be a commutative ring.

Definition 3.2

(Objects of finite presentation)
.

A ring
A\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is said to be of
finite presentation
(or
finitely presented
) if the canonical morphism

\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(A,B_{i})\to{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(A,\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})

is an isomorphism, for all filtered colimits
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i}
of
R
-algebras. An analogous definition is given for objects in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.

We would like to recall explicitly, as this will play a key role in the forthcoming discussion, that the generators of the polynomial algebra with coefficients in
R
can be obtained as the image, via the tensor product functor
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
, of the generators of polynomial algebra with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
. So, we have the canonical isomorphism

(3.3)

R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R\,.

For
R\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}
, let us denote by
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
the
free algebra
with coefficients in
R
, with the property that
R
is in the centre of the algebra. Concretely,

R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle:=\left\{\sum a_{w}w\,|\,w\text{ is a word in the variables }x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\right\}

In the noncommutative case the computation of the free product functor
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}R
on the above free algebras with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
gives rise to the family of algebras

(3.4)

R\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}:={\mathbb{Z}}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}R.

One might expect that
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\cong R\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. However, for a general
R
, this is
not
the case, even when
R
is commutative. This happens because the generators of (
3.4
) do not commute with all the elements of
R
.

Example 3.3
.

Consider the case
R={\mathbb{Z}}[x]
. Then,

R\{y\}={\mathbb{Z}}[y]\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}R={\mathbb{Z}}[y]\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Z}}[x]\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x,y\rangle.

In particular, the image of
R\to R\{y\}
does not lie in the center, whereas

R\langle y\rangle=R[y]\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[x,y]\neq{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x,y\rangle\cong R\{y\}.

Proposition 3.4
.

We have the following.

(i)

A ring
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a quotient of
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

(ii)

A ring
A\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a quotient of
{\mathbb{Z}}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
by a finitely generated ideal.

Proof.

(i)

This result is well-known. However, we provide a full proof for reader’s convenience.

Since
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is Noetherian, all its ideals are finitely generated. Suppose

A\cong\frac{{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}{I}\cong{\rm Coeq\,}({\mathbb{Z}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])

where
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
are ring homomorphisms such that
{\rm Im\,}(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})
generates
3
3

3

For example, if
I=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
then we can consider
\phi_{1}(y_{i})=1
for all
i
and
\phi_{2}(y_{i})=1-f_{i}
. So,
(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})(y)\in I
for all
y
and the generators of
I
are in the image.
the ideal
I
. Then,

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(A,\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\rm Coeq\,}({\mathbb{Z}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]),\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\\
\cong{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\mathbb{Z}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}],\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1,*},\phi_{2,*}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}],\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\right)\\
\cong{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\},\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1,*},\phi_{2,*}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\},\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\right)

where the last isomorphism comes from the adjunction between the category of sets and the category of commutative rings. But then we have

(3.5)

\displaystyle{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\},\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1,*},\phi_{2,*}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\},\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}B_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\},B_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1,*},\phi_{2,*}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\},B_{i})\right)\,.

Here we are using the fact that finite sets are finitely presented objects in the category of sets and that filtered colimits of sets commute with equalizers. It then follows that

\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\},B_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1,*},\phi_{2,*}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sets}}}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\},B_{i})\right)\\
\cong\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\mathbb{Z}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}],B_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1,*},\phi_{2,*}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}],B_{i})\right)\\
\cong\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\Bigl({\rm Coeq\,}({\mathbb{Z}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]),B_{i}\Bigr)\\
\cong\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(A,B_{i})

where we just performed similar steps to those in the first chain of isomorphisms above, in reversed order.
On the other hand, it is clear that if
A
does not have a presentation as a quotient of
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
then it must have infinitely many generators and the isomorphism (
3.5
) does not hold, because
{\rm Hom\,}
out of an infinite set would have to commute with filtered colimits, which it does not. Hence
A
is not of finite presentation.

(ii)

The proof for the noncommutative case is analogous to that for the commutative case, modulo the following changes:

•

replace
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots x_{n}]
with
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x_{1},\ldots x_{n}\rangle
;

•

observe that the algebras
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
are not Noetherian, hence require that the ideal
I
be
finitely generated
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

As a straightforward consequence of Proposition
3.4
we have the following.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
R
be a commutative
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra. We have the following.

(i)

The ring
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a quotient of
R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
by a finitely generated ideal.

(ii)

The ring
A\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a quotient of
R\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
by a finitely generated ideal.

Proof.

(i)

The claim for a general
R
is obtained from the case
R={\mathbb{Z}}
— i.e., Proposition
3.4
(i) — via the tensor product functor (base change) and formula (
3.3
).

(ii)

The claim for a general
R
is obtained from the case
R={\mathbb{Z}}
— i.e., Proposition
3.4
(ii) — via the free product functor and formula (
3.4
).

∎

Let us now discuss the corresponding notion for morphisms.

Definition 3.6

(Morphisms of finite presentation)
.

A morphism
A\to B\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is said to be of
finite presentation
(or
finitely presented
) if the canonical morphism

\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}}(B,C_{i})\to{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}}(B,\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}C_{i})

is an isomorphism, for all filtered colimits
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}C_{i}
of
A
-algebras.
An analogous definition is given for morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.

Remark 3.7
.

Note that an object
A\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if the structure map
R\to A
is a morphism of finite presentation, and a morphism
A\to B
is of finite presentation if and only if
B
is an object of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
. The same is true for commutative rings. Therefore, when it comes to finite presentation, the use of the terminology for morphisms and objects is compatible. Henceforth, we will often tacitly identify the two notions.

The notions of finite presentation in the commutative and noncommutative cases are formally the same. Therefore, it is natural to ask whether the two are compatible; namely, whether a morphism in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is of finite presentation as a morphism in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Perhaps surprisingly, this matter turns out to be very subtle, and the answer is in the negative, as the following example demonstrates.

Example 3.8
.

Let
R
be the commutative ring of integral polynomials in countably many variables,
R:={\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},x_{2},\dots]
, and consider the polynomial ring
S:=R[y]
in one variable with coefficients in
R
. Clearly,
S
is commutative and the canonical inclusion morphism
R\to S
is of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, since
S
is a free finitely generated commutative
R
-algebra.

As an object in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
,
S
can be realised as the quotient of
R\{y\}={\mathbb{Z}}\langle y\rangle\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}R

by the ideal

I:=\{(x_{j}y-yx_{j})\ |\ j\in{\mathbb{N}}\}.

Namely,

S\cong\frac{R\{y\}}{I}.

Let us emphasise that the variable
y
of
R\{y\}
does not commute with the variables
x_{j}
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, of
R
.

We claim that the morphism
f:R\to S
is
not
of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Indeed, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
define
I_{n}:=\{(x_{j}y-yx_{j})\in I\ |\ j\leq n\}
.
Then the canonical map

\underset{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}\left(S,\frac{R\{y\}}{I_{n}}\right)\to{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}\left(S,\underset{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}}\frac{R\{y\}}{I_{n}}\right)\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}\left(S,S\right)

fails to be an isomorphism because the identity
S\to S
does not factor through any of the quotients on the left-hand-side. In particular, this shows that
S
is not finitely presented as an object in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, recall Remark
3.7
.

In plain English, the above example tells us that the incompatibility between the commutative and noncommutative notions of finite presentation boils down to the fact that the condition on the commutation of the
{\rm Hom\,}
functor with filtered colimits is required on two different classes of diagrams.

Now, in Example
3.8
the (commutative) ring
R
does not map into the center of
R\{y\}
. This leads to a violation of the finite presentation property — as soon as
R
is not finitely generated — because in order to write
R[y]
as a quotient of
R\{y\}
one must add infinitely many relations to ensure that in the resulting quotient
y
commutes with all elements.
There are (at least) two possible ways to reconcile the notions of finite presentation in the commutative and noncommutative settings. One way is to restrict oneself to the case where both
R
and
R\{y\}
(or more general version thereof) are finitely generated over
{\mathbb{Z}}
; for example, this holds for discrete algebras (also known as
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebras). Another way is to consider the category of
R
-algebras for which
R
maps into their centre. The latter opens up an intermediate situation which we will now examine.

Motivated by the above discussion, given a commutative ring
R
we define
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
be the subcategory of
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
given by

(3.6)

{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}:=\{S\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\ |\ R\to S\ \text{has image in the centre $Z(S)$ of S}\}\,.

By definition the inclusion
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\subset{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
as categories is full. As it turns out, despite
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
contains noncommutative rings, we have following result.

Proposition 3.9
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

A\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a quotient of
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
by a finitely generated ideal.

(ii)

A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.

(iii)

In general, objects of finite presentation of
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
may not be of finite presentation as objects of
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.

Proof.

(i)

The claim follows by arguing as in Proposition
3.4
and using the fact that all objects of
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
can be written as suitable quotients of inductive limits of the algebras
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
. We will revisit this in more detail in the next subsection.

(ii)

Suppose that
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation as an object of
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, then

A\cong\frac{R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}{I}

for some
n
and some (finitely generated) ideal
I
.

Let
I_{n}
be the ideal generated by the elements

(3.7)

\{x_{j}x_{k}-x_{k}x_{j}\ |\ j,k\in\{1,\dots,n\},\ j<k\}

in
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
.
Since

R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\cong\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle}{I_{n}}\,,

then
A
can be written as the quotient

A\cong\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle}{\widetilde{I}}

where the ideal
\widetilde{I}
is the preimage of
I
through the canonical map
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\to R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. Hence,
A
is finitely presented in
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
because
\widetilde{I}
is clearly finitely generated.

Now suppose that
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is finitely presented as an object of
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. This means that one can write

A\cong\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle}{I}\,,

where
I
is some finitely generated ideal. Then one has

(3.8)

A\cong A^{\rm ab\,}\cong\left(\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle}{I}\right)^{\rm ab\,}\cong\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle^{\rm ab\,}}{I^{\rm ab\,}}=\frac{R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}{I^{\rm ab\,}}\,.

Here to obtain the third isomorphism we used the fact that the functor
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})^{\rm ab\,}
is left adjoint to
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
:

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(A^{\rm ab\,},S)\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(A,S)\\
\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}({\rm Coeq\,}(R\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle),S)\\
\cong{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(R\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rangle,S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle,S)\right)\\
\cong{\rm eq\,}\left({\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(R\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rangle^{\rm ab\,},S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle^{\rm ab\,},S)\right)\\
\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}({\rm Coeq\,}(R\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rangle^{\rm ab\,}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}}{{\rightrightarrows}}R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle^{\rm ab\,}),S)\\
\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}\left(\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle^{\rm ab\,}}{I^{\rm ab\,}},S\right).

Formula (
3.8
) implies that
A
is finitely presented as an object of
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, which concludes the proof.

(iii)

A counterexample is provided by
Example
3.8
, upon observing that
R[y]
is also an element in
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and using part (ii) of this proposition.

∎

Proposition
3.9
(iii) has profound implications for the noncommutative geometry over
R
, and warrants a remark.

Remark 3.10
.

In algebraic geometry the open localizations of a commutative ring are taken to be morphisms of finite presentation (in most topologies), because, geometrically, this means that they are morphisms of finite dimension between the associated affine schemes.
However, as illustrated by Example
3.8
, the notions of finite presentation in the commutative and noncommutative settings are not compatible.
Indeed, the Zariski open localizations used, say, in the theory of schemes or in the theory of algebraic varieties are no longer of finite presentation as morphisms of
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
for a general commutative ring
R
, which makes noncommutative geometry fundamentally incompatible with the theory of schemes, when the notion of finite presentation comes into play. Namely, in our setting, if one were to use morphisms of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
to define the Zariski topology, then the associated spectrum built out of this theory would be a singleton — cf. Proposition
5.21
.

3.1.2
Reformulation in terms of algebraic theories

The best way to explain the incompatibility described so far between the notions of finite presentation is perhaps to think in terms of algebraic theories in the sense of Lawvere
[
39
]
. In this subsection we briefly explain how this is done.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be an essentially small category with finite coproducts. The
algebraic category
of
{\mathscr{C}}
is the full subcategory of the category of pre-sheaves on
{\mathscr{C}}

{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})={\mathbf{Func}}^{\times}({\mathscr{C}}^{\rm op\,},{\mathbf{Sets}})\subset{\mathbf{Func}}({\mathscr{C}}^{\rm op\,},{\mathbf{Sets}})={\mathbf{Psh}}({\mathscr{C}})

where
{\mathbf{Func}}^{\times}
denotes the class of functors that preserve finite products (of
{\mathscr{C}}^{\rm op\,}
). Therefore, objects of
{\mathbf{Func}}^{\times}({\mathscr{C}}^{\rm op\,},{\mathbf{Sets}})
are contravariant functors
{\mathscr{F}}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\rm op\,}\to{\mathbf{Sets}}
such that the canonical morphism

{\mathscr{F}}(A\coprod B)\to{\mathscr{F}}(A)\times{\mathscr{F}}(B)

is an isomorphism.

In the same way it is possible to show that objects of
{\mathbf{Psh}}({\mathscr{C}})
can be written as arbitrary colimits in
{\mathbf{Psh}}({\mathscr{C}})
of representable pre-sheaves, one can also show that objects of
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})
are precisely those pre-sheaves that can be written as
sifted colimits
4
4

4

A colimit
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}A_{i}
is said
sifted
if the partially ordered set
I
is such that the diagonal map
I\to I\times I
is cofinal. For example, filtered colimits are sifted, but not all sifted colimits are filtered.
of representable pre-sheaves.
For this reason, the reader should think of the objects of
{\mathscr{C}}
as the free and finitely generated objects of some class of algebraic structures and of the objects of
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})
as obtained from the free ones by considering infinite unions and quotients by relations. Almost all categories of algebraic objects can be presented as
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})
for a suitable
{\mathscr{C}}
. We provide below some basic examples.

Example 3.11
.

•

If
{\mathscr{C}}
is the category of finite sets, then
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})
is the category of sets.

•

If
{\mathscr{C}}
is the category of finitely generated and free abelian groups,
i.e.
abelian groups of the form
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})\cong{\mathbf{Ab}}
. This is (roughly) equivalent to say that any abelian group can be written as a quotient of a free abelian group,
i.e.
it has a presentation in terms of (possibly infinitely many) generators and relations.

•

The previous example can be generalized immediately to left (or right) modules over any ring. Indeed, if
R
is any ring and
{\mathscr{C}}
is the category of finitely generated and free left (or right) modules then
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})\cong{}_{R}{\mathbf{Mod}}
(resp.
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathscr{C}})\cong{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
).

In the spirit of the previous subsection, let us focus our attention on the algebraic theories of rings. Let us start with the case
R={\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
{\mathbf{Poly}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the full subcategory spanned by polynomial algebras in finitely many variables,
i.e.
the algebras
{\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. It is well-known that

{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathbf{Poly}}_{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}.

One way to prove this equivalence is by applying
[
1
, Theorem 6.9]
noticing that
{\mathbf{Poly}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is precisely the subcategory of perfectly presented generators of
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, in the sense of
[
1
, Definition 5.3]
.
Intuitively, this is equivalent to saying that every commutative ring can be presented as a quotient of an algebra of polynomials in an arbitrary number of variables by some relations.

The noncommutative situation is very similar.
Let
{\mathbf{Free}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the full subcategory spanned by free algebras in finitely many variables,
i.e.
the algebras
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
introduced so far. It is well-known that

{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathbf{Free}}_{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Again the equivalence can be easily reduced to an application of
[
1
, Theorem 6.9]
.

Let us now recast the case of a general (commutative)
R
as an algebraic category. Since the generators of the polynomial algebra with coefficients in
R
can be obtained as the image, via the tensor product functor
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R
, of the polynomial algebras with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
— see (
3.3
) — the slice category
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
of
R
-algebras is the algebraic category associated to
{\mathbf{Poly}}_{R}
.

Let us define the category
{\mathbf{Free}}_{R}\subset{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
as the category spanned by the free noncommutative algebras in finitely many variables over
R
,
i.e.
the algebras of the form
R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
. One might wonder whether these objects generate the category
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
; this turns out not to be the case, because
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
contains a lot of objects for which
R
does not map into the center, see
e.g.
Example
3.3
and Example
3.8
. Consequently, the category
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is not the algebraic category associated to the category
{\mathbf{Free}}_{R}
of free algebras over
R
, because it contains algebras that cannot be written as quotient of free algebras in any number of variables. Below we complete the analysis of Example
3.3
, to impress on this crucial phenomenon.

Example 3.12
.

Consider the inclusion
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]\to{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x,y\rangle
. The ring on the LHS is commutative, whereas in the target
xy\neq yx
. Hence,
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
is not mapped into the center of the target ring. In particular, the
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
-algebra
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x,y\rangle
that cannot be written as a quotient of

{\mathbb{Z}}[x]\langle\{y_{k}\}_{k\in K}\rangle

for any set of variables, where
K
is an arbitrary indexing set.

Inspired by the above example, and in the spirit of what done in the previous subsection, one turns to
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, looking for an intermediate situation. One can show that
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is, in fact, the algebraic category
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathbf{Free}}_{R})
:

{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathbf{Free}}_{R})\cong{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\,.

Finally, in order to view
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
as an algebraic category, we denote by
{\mathbf{N}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Free}}_{R}
the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
comprising algebras
R\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
defined in accordance with (
3.4
).
Then we have that

{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathbf{N}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Free}}_{R})\cong{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}.

Again, these claims can be proved by a straightforward application of
[
1
, Theorem 6.9]
.

Remark 3.13
.

We should like to point out that the equivalence
{\mathbf{P}}_{\Sigma}({\mathbf{N}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Free}}_{R})\cong{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is true and makes sense also when
R
is noncommutative, whereas the category
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
does not make sense (or at least is a very unnatural object to consider) for noncommutative rings.

3.2  Homotopical algebras

As in the previous subsection,
R\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}
is a fixed commutative
{\mathbb{Z}}
-algebra.
After recalling the theory of discrete rings over
R
, we now would like to provide a similar discussion in the homotopical setting. The categories
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
have formal properties that are very different from the ones of discrete objects, if they are considered as bare categories,
i.e.
without considering their model structures that enrich them to
\infty
-categories.
The main difference between the discrete and the homotopical settings is that homotopy categories of model categories usually do not have all limits and colimits. In particular, they usually do not have all pullbacks nor all pushouts.
Nevertheless, it is possible to construct objects that are very similar to pullbacks and pushouts but lack the universal property
5
5

5

To restore the universal properties of these constructions and get rid of the “negligible indeterminacy” of homotopy pushouts and homotopy pullbacks, it is necessary to use the language of
\infty
-categories. However, there is no need for us to discuss such matters in detail, in that it is possible to define homotopy pullbacks and homotopy pushouts directly in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
; these pullbacks and pushouts will just lack universal properties characterising as
\infty
-categorical limits or colimits.
. These objects are called
homotopy pullbacks
and
homotopy pushouts
and we shall define them in the framework of model categories
6
6

6

Homotopy limits and homotopy colimits can be considered in all the different settings where homotopy theory can be developed. We presented our homotopy categories via model categories, therefore we will discuss the definition of homotopy limits and colimits only for model categories.
.

Let us start by recalling the following definition.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
F:{\mathscr{C}}\to{\mathscr{D}}
be a functor between model categories. The
left derived functor
of
F

Notice that the notion of derived functor introduced in Definition
3.14
is compatible with the notion of derived functors of a Quillen adjoint pair introduced before.
In general, derived functors may not exist because Kan extensions of functors do not always exist. The notion of derived functor permits us to define homotopy limits and homotopy colimits.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a model category
7
7

7

We recall that by definition model categories have all limits and colimits, in the
1
-categorical sense.
,
I
a poset and consider the functor
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\leftarrow}}_{i\in I}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$}):{\mathscr{C}}^{I}\to{\mathscr{C}}
. Then, the
homotopy limit functor
is the derived functor
{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\leftarrow}}_{i\in I}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
. Similarly, the
homotopy colimit functor
is the derived functor
{\mathbb{L}}\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
of the functor
\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
.

In Definition
3.15
we have taken for granted that a model structure on
{\mathscr{C}}
induces a model structure on the category of diagrams
{\mathscr{C}}^{I}
. We refer to
[
28
, Chapter 13]
for an exposition of the standard methods to endow
{\mathscr{C}}^{I}
with model structures.

Proposition 3.16
.

The categories
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
have all homotopy limits and homotopy colimits.

Proof.

This is a well known result for which we provide a brief justification.
We notice that the categories of simplicial rings and simplicial commutative rings have all limits and colimits because they are categories of functors valued in complete and cocomplete categories and therefore limits and colimits are computed objectwise. Therefore, we only need to check that the limit and colimit functors are Quillen functors, which implies that they are derivable in the sense of Definition
3.15
. Since both
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are combinatorial model categories, for any diagram
I
we can endow
({\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R})^{I}
(resp.
({\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R})^{I}
) with the projective (resp. injective) model structure making all the required adjunctions Quillen. Once again, we refer to
[
28
, Chapter 13]
for further details about these constructions.
∎

Later on, we will be interested in computing homotopy pushouts (of homotopy rings) and homotopy pullbacks (of affine noncommutative spaces). By Definition
3.15
the homotopy pushouts in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are given by the derived functor of the pushout in
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, respectively. By the description of pushouts in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, we can deduce that the pushouts in
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are given by computing the commutative simplicial ring

(X^{\bullet}\otimes_{Y^{\bullet}}Z^{\bullet})^{n}=X^{n}\otimes_{Y_{n}}Z^{n}

for
X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet},Z^{\bullet}\in{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Whereas, the the pushouts in
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are given by the simplicial ring

(X^{\bullet}\ast_{Y^{\bullet}}Z^{\bullet})^{n}=X^{n}\ast_{Y_{n}}Z^{n}

for
X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet},Z^{\bullet}\in{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, see
[
57
, Section 14.8]
. Therefore, the homotopy pushouts in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are computed as

A\otimes_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}C

and

A\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}C\,,

respectively, and Proposition
3.16
ensures that these derived functors exist.

To define finitely presented objects in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
we need to recall another concept from homotopy theory. We recall that both
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are simplicial model categories. This means that given two objects
X,Y\in{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
(or
X,Y\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
) there is a natural simplicial set of morphisms between them

\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(X,Y).

To describe the simplicial set
\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(X,Y)
we notice that the categories
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
satisfy the hypothesis of
[
51
, Proposition 4.2]
and therefore we can use their formula to define it. So, for any set
X
and any
A\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
(resp.
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
) we define

A\cdot X=\mathop{\Huge{{\ast}}}_{x\in X}A\ \ \ \ \text{(resp. }A\cdot X=\bigotimes_{x\in X}A\text{)}

and then for any
X^{\bullet}\in{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Sets}}
and
A^{\bullet}\in{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
(resp.
A^{\bullet}\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
) we define the simplicial set

(A\otimes X)^{n}=A^{n}\cdot X^{n}.

With this notation, we define

\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}_{{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(X,Y)^{n}={\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(X\otimes\Delta_{n},Y)

for
X,Y\in{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, and similarly in the case of
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. One can show that the description just given for the mapping spaces of
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
implies that the inclusion
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is not fully faithful.

We also recall that the category of simplicial sets has a natural model structure defined by Quillen (see
e.g.

[
35
, Chapter 2]
), therefore it makes sense to ask for the existence of the derived functor that would make the following diagram commutative

where
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Sets}}={\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Sets}})
is the homotopy category of
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Sets}}
with respect to the Quillen model structure, that is equivalent to the homotopy category of topological spaces with the standard model structure.
It turns out that the derived functor
{\mathbb{R}}\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}
exists and can be computed as

{\mathbb{R}}\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(X,Y)=\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(Q(X),Q(Y))

where
Q(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
is the cofibrant replacement functor. One important property of the derived hom-spaces is that

\pi_{0}({\mathbb{R}}\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(X,Y))={\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(Q(X),Q(Y)).

Definition 3.17
.

An object
X\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
(resp.
X\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
) is said
homotopically of finite presentation
if the natural map

{\mathbb{L}}\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}{\mathbb{R}}\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(X,Y_{i})\to{\mathbb{R}}\underline{{\rm Hom\,}}(X,{\mathbb{L}}\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{i\in I}Y_{i})

is an equivalence for all filtered systems
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.

Proposition 3.18
.

1.

X\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a retract of a finite cell of finitely generated free algebras.

2.

X\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation if and only if it is a retract of a finite cell of finitely generated polynomial algebras.

Before proving the proposition we briefly explain the terminology used. We refer to
[
59
, Definition 1.2.3.4]
for a more detailed discussion and to
[
59
, Proposition 1.2.3.5]
for a more general version of our Proposition
3.18
. We say that an object
X\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is a
strict finite cell of finitely generated free algebras
if there exists a finite sequence of morphisms

R=X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}=X

and for any
i
a push-out square

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.17276pt}{-16.56737pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -1.62 -22.92)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We say that
X
is a
finite cell of finitely generated free algebras
if it is equivalent to a strict finite cell of finitely generated free algebras. The same definition is given for objects of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
replacing the free algebras with polynomial algebras.

Proof of Proposition
3.18

.

We first notice that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
(resp.
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
) are compactly generated and the objects
R\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
(resp.
R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
) are a set of
\omega
-compact and
\omega
-small generators. Therefore,
[
59
, Proposition 1.2.3.5 (4)]
, applies to both
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. More precisely, we can use the same reasoning of
[
59
, Proposition 1.2.3.5 (4)]
to show that all the objects
R\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
(resp.
R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
) are homotopically finitely presented. Once this is proved it is easy to check that reatracts of finite cells of the
\omega
-compact and
\omega
-small generators are finitely generated and, conversely, every finitely presented object is a retract of such a finite cell.
∎

Recall that there are fully faithful inclusions
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\subset{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\subset{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
where the classical categories are embedded as discrete objects. So, it is natural to ask what is the relation between the classical notion of finite presentation and the homotopical version of finite presentation. It turns out that the homotopical version is strictly stronger than the classical version. We briefly explain why this is the case in the next example.

Example 3.19
.

Suppose that
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is of finite presentation (in the noncommutative setting we can reason in a similar fashion). This means that we can write

A\cong{\rm Coker\,}(R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to R[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}])

in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Now, the operation of taking cokernel does not exist in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and is replaced by the homotopical cokernel (also known as cofiber or cone). So, we can consider the object

{\mathbb{L}}{\rm Coker\,}(R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to R[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}])

that is well-defined in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Notice that it is always true that

\pi_{0}({\mathbb{L}}{\rm Coker\,}(R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to R[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]))\cong A

but, in general, for specific examples of
A
,
\pi_{n}({\mathbb{L}}{\rm Coker\,}(R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to R[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]))\neq 0
for
n>0
. Such examples are obtained by quotienting
R[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]
by an ideal whose generators do not form a regular sequence. Therefore, the (finite) presentation given by the homotopical cokernel above is not a presentation of
A
in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
, but it presents another object whose homotopy groups are not concentrated in degree
0
. This means that
A
has a different presentation in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and this different presentation may be finite or not. We do not provide an explicit example of an object of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
for which its presentation in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is not finite anymore, but the reader should be convinced that it is possible to manufacture one.

We would like to emphasise that although Example
3.19
shows that being homotopically of finite presentation is different from being classically of finite presentation for a morphism of commutative rings, for many important classes of morphisms the two notions are equivalent. Since the mismatch between the two notions is given by higher cohomology groups, for flat maps the two notions of finite presentation agree. Therefore, for example, for smooth or étale maps of commutative rings, being of finite presentation or homotopical finite presentation is equivalent.

Moreover, the above discussion implies that in the homotopical world the same incompatibility between the notions of finite presentation in the commutative and noncommutative settings remains. Indeed, since for a morphism of commutative rings being homotopically of finite presentation implies being classically of finite presentation, then being not classically of finite presentation implies being not homotopically of finite presentation. Thus, we have see in Example
3.8
that there are commutative rings
R
for which the algebras
R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
are not of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and hence these are not of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. We do not dwell further into the study of the homotopical finite presentation of morphisms of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
because the main constructions of this work get rid of the hypothesis of finite presentations on the morphisms.

We will also use the following property of the homotopical monoidal Dold-Kan correspondence.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let

{\mathbb{L}}N:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{G}}{\mathbf{A}}_{R}^{\leq 0}):({\mathbb{L}}N:)^{-1}

and

({\mathbb{R}}\Gamma)^{-1}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{G}}{\mathbf{A}}_{R}^{\leq 0}):{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma.

be the adjunctions given by the homotopical monoidal Dold-Kan equivalence,
cf.
Proposition
2.2
.
Then,

{\mathbb{L}}N(A\ast_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}B)\cong{\mathbb{L}}N(A)\ast_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{L}}N(B)

and

{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(C\ast_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}D)\cong{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(C)\ast_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(D)

for all
A,B\in{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{SRings}}_{R})
and
C,D\in{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{G}}{\mathbf{A}}_{R}^{\leq 0})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.2
both
{\mathbb{L}}N
and
{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma
commute with the tensor product. The free product of algebras can be written in terms of direct sums and tensor products, therefore both
{\mathbb{L}}N
and
{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma
commute with both these operation because they are both equivalences. Or, equivalently, we can notice that Quillen equivalences commute with homotopy limits and homotopy colimits in general, so all the above functors commute with homotopy pushouts.
∎

3.3  Modules over homotopical rings

We now consider
R\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. So
R
is a fixed, not necessarily commutative, homotopical ring in this subsection. We briefly discuss the category of (left, right or bi-) modules over an object
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
(or
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
if
R
is commutative). It is convenient to work at the level of model categories. We can think of an object
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
as a simplicial ring; the theory of monoidal categories allows one to associate to
A
the category of simplicial left modules
{}_{A}{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Mod}}
, simplicial right modules
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
and simplicial bimodules
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}
over
A
. All these categories inherit a model structure from the model structure on
{}_{R}{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Mod}}
,
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
, and
{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{R}
, respectively. For each of these categories one can then construct the associated homotopy category:

{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}:={\mathbf{Ho}}({}_{A}{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Mod}}),\ \ {\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}:={\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}),\ \ {\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}:={\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}).

For later convenience, let us discuss the relation between a morphism
f:A\to B
in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and the corresponding induced functors between the homotopy categories just introduced. The morphism
f
induces the following adjunctions:

(3.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{L}}f^{*}=B\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}\leftrightarrows{}_{B}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}:f_{*}\,,

(3.10)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{L}}f^{*}=(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}:f_{*}\,,

(3.11)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{L}}f^{*}=(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{B}:f_{*}\,,

(3.12)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{L}}f^{*}=(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}:{\mathbb{R}}f_{*}\,,

where the right adjoints are obtained via the morphism
f
by forgetting the
B
-module (resp.
B
-algebra) structure. These functors will play a fundamental role in all that follows. As a final remark, we notice that when we write
f_{*}
in place of
{\mathbb{R}}f_{*}
we mean that the derived functor functor is trivial, in the sense that the left Kan extension of Definition
3.14
is a natural isomorphism.

4  The noncommutative spectrum

This section is the core of this paper. Using derived geometry, we will introduce a robust notion of spectrum for homotopical rings. This will be achieved by defining a Grothendieck topology on the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm op\,}
, in analogy with the Zariski topology used in scheme theory.
We will argue that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm op\,}
is a good candidate for a noncommutative replacement of the category of affine schemes. Denoting by
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}:={\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm op\,}
the category of
derived noncommutative affine schemes
, let us introduce the formal duality functor (
i.e.
the formal contravariant functor
mapping an object to itself and a morphism to the opposite morphism)

(4.1)

\displaystyle\textrm{Spec}:

\displaystyle\;{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle A\mapsto{\rm Spec}(A)=:X\,.

In the remainder of this section we will provide a geometric construction of the functor
{\rm Spec}
that closely mimics the classical construction of the spectrum of a commutative ring given by Grothendieck. Namely, we will define a Grothendieck topology on
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
that almost precisely generalises the (classical) Zariski topology of scheme theory.

Let us begin by giving a preliminary definition.

Definition 4.1
.

A morphism
f:A\to B
in the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is said to be a

•

homological epimorphism
if the canonical map
B\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\to B
is an equivalence;

•

homotopical epimorphism
if the codiagonal map
B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\to B
is an equivalence.

The next proposition motivates the choice of homotopical epimorphisms as noncommutative localizations and guarantees that our construction will be ‘compatible’ with the classical one when applied to commutative rings. For further details, we refer to Section
5.1
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
f
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

The morphism
f
is a homological epimorphism.

(b)

The morphism
f
is a homotopical epimorphism.

(c)

The functor
{\mathbb{R}}f_{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
is fully faithful.

(d)

The functor
f_{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is fully faithful.

(e)

The functor
f_{*}:{}_{B}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}\to{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}
is fully faithful.

(f)

The functor
f_{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{B}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

The equivalence between assertions
(a)
and
(b)
was proved in
[
23
, Theorem 4.4]
. Observe that
[
23
, Theorem 4.4]
is stated for unbounded dg-algebras but it directly implies our statement in the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Indeed, the homotopy category of connective dg-algebras
{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{DGA}}^{\leq 0})
sits fully faithfully into the homotopy category of unbounded dg-algebras
{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{DGA}})
and, since the free product is a left derived functor, the free product of connective dg-algebras is always connective, independently of whether it is considered as objects in
{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{DGA}}^{\leq 0})
or in
{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{DGA}})
. So, for a morphism
f:A\to B
in
{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{DGA}}^{\leq 0})
the condition

B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}B

is equivalent to require that the same condition holds in
{\mathbf{Ho}}({\mathbf{DGA}})
. Then, by Lemma
3.20
the functor
{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma
commutes with free products and therefore

B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}B\ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ {\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(B)\ast_{{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(A)}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma(B)\,;

since
{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma
is a monoidal equivalence, its quasi-inverse
{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}
is strongly monoidal, too. Therefore, the isomorphism

{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}(B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}(B)
implies
{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}(B)\ast_{{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}(A)}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{L}}\Gamma^{-1}(B)
;
this gives us that also in the connective case the notions of homological and homotopical epimorphism coincide, because
{\mathbb{R}}\Gamma
is also compatible with the (derived) tensor product.

For the equivalence of the assertion
(a)
with each of
(d)
,
(e)
, and
(f)
, we notice that the canonical morphism

B\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\to B

is the counit of the adjunctions (
3.9
), (
3.10
), and (
3.11
), respectively, because it is a map of bimodules. Then, the requirement that the counit transformation of an adjunction is a natural isomorphism is equivalent to the right adjoint being fully faithful. Similarly, the equivalence between
(b)
and
(c)
is obtained by observing that the codiagonal morphism

B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\to B

is the counit of the adjunction (
3.12
), and asking that it is a natural isomorphism is equivalent to ask that the right adjoint is fully faithful. This completes the proof.
∎

4.1  The formal homotopy Zariski topology

In order to define a Grothendieck topology on
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
we first need to specify appropriate notions of
open embedding
and
cover
for the topology. More explicitly:

Definition 4.3
.

We call
formal homotopy Zariski topology
the topology
\mathscr{T}_{{\rm Zar\,}}
whose open embeddings are formal homotopical Zariski open embedding and the covers are formal covers, where:

•

A morphism
f:{\rm Spec}(B)\to{\rm Spec}(A)
in
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is said to be a
formal homotopical Zariski open embedding
if it is a homotopical epimorphism in the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

•

A finite family of formal homotopical Zariski open embedding
\{f_{i}:{\rm Spec}(B_{i})\to{\rm Spec}(A)\}_{i}
is called a
formal cover
if the family of functors

\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B_{i}}\}_{i}
is conservative.

Proposition 4.4
.

The formal homotopy Zariski topology on
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
defines a Grothendieck topology.

Proof.

To prove our claim we have to check that three conditions are satisfied.

1.

That if a morphism
f\in{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is an isomorphism, then it is also a homotopical Zariski open embeddings. This is clearly true.

2.

That, given a formal homotopical Zariski cover
\{U_{i}\to X\}
and a morphism
Y\to X
, the pullback family
\{U_{i}\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}Y\to Y\}
is formal homotopical Zariski cover. To this end, if we write
X={\rm Spec}(A)
,
Y={\rm Spec}(B)
, and
U_{i}={\rm Spec}(A_{i})
then
U_{i}\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}Y={\rm Spec}(A_{i}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)
. So, since homotopy pushouts preserve homotopy epimorphisms, then the fact that the morphisms
f_{i}:A\to A_{i}
are homotopy epimorphisms imply that the morphisms
f_{i}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B:B\to A_{i}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B
are homotopy epimorphisms as well. Furthermore, we have that the family for functors
\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A_{i}}\}
is conservative by hypothesis. Let
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}
and assume that there is a morphism
C\to C^{\prime}
that induces isomorphisms

C\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}B\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{i}\simeq C^{\prime}\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{i}\,

for all
i
. We need to check that
C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{i}\simeq C^{\prime}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{i}
. But by the dual of the (homotopical) pullback lemma (see
[
40
, Lemma 4.4.2.1]
), we get that in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}B\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}}

the fact that the two small squares are homotopy pushouts imply that also the outer square is a homotopy pushout. Therefore, we get a (non-canonical, up to a contractible space of choices) isomorphism

C\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}B\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\cong C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}.

And since the same is true for
C^{\prime}
, we get isomorphisms

C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\cong C^{\prime}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}

for all
i
. By hypothesis this implies that
C
and
C^{\prime}
are isomorphic as objects of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
; but the forgetful functor
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
is conservative, therefore the isomorphism lifts to an isomorphism of objects of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}
.

3.

Finally it remains to check the stability of covers by composition. Given a homotopical Zariski cover
\{U_{i}\to X\}
and, for every
i
, homotopical Zariski covers
\{V_{i,j}\to U_{i}\}
of
U_{i}
, this amounts to check that the family
\{V_{i,j}\to X\}
is a homotopical Zariski cover of
X
. Again, let us adopt the notation
X={\rm Spec}(A)
,
U_{i}={\rm Spec}(A_{i})
, and
V_{i,j}={\rm Spec}(B_{i,j})
, and let us work algebraically in the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
It is easy to check that the composition of two homotopy epimorphisms is a homotopy epimorphism, so the composite morphism
V_{i,j}\to U_{i}\to X
is a homotopy epimorphism for all
i,j
. It remains to check the conservativity condition of the cover. Let
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
and assume that there is a morphism
C\to C^{\prime}
that induces isomorphisms

C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i,j}\simeq C^{\prime}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i,j}\,

for all
i,j
. We use again the (dual of the) homotopical pullback lemma (
cf.

[
40
, Lemma 4.4.2.1]
) to deduce that in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A_{i}}B_{i,j}}

the outer square is a homotopy pushout square. And the same with
C
replaced by
C^{\prime}
. This gives isomorphisms

C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B_{i,j}\cong C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A_{i}}B_{i,j},\ \ C^{\prime}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B_{i,j}\cong C^{\prime}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A_{i}}B_{i,j}.

So, the data of these isomorphism and the fact that for all
j
the family
\{V_{i,j}\to U_{i}\}
is a cover imply that for all
i
we have isomorphisms

C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\cong C^{\prime}\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A_{i}\,.

This family of isomorphisms plus the fact that
\{U_{i}\to X\}
is a cover imply that

C\cong C^{\prime}

as claimed.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition
4.4
can be easily generalised as follows.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
R\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, not necessarily discrete. The formal homotopy Zariski topology on
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
restricts to a topology on
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.

Proof.

In the proof of Proposition
4.4
only formal properties of homotopical epimorphisms have been used: the base ring
{\mathbb{Z}}
plays no role. It is straightforward to check that the same arguments work in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

We point out another striking difference between the commutative and the noncommutative situations. In (derived) algebraic geometry, for affine morphisms of schemes the Base Change Theorem holds. In this situation, the theorem takes the following form. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B}

be a pushout square of commutative algebras, and let
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{C}
, then
M\otimes_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}C\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B
. Of course, in this situation, the theorem is just a basic cancellation property of the tensor product functor. This theorem has several consequences in terms of relating the geometry of
{\rm Spec}(A)
to the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
. But in the noncommutative situation, pushouts are computed via the free product of algebras. So, in this case we have that pushouts of algebras are given by diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B}

therefore, the base change theorem would have the form of an isomorphism

M\otimes_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B

for all
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{C}
, that is not true in general, not even with
M=C
. This creates extra complications in the relations between the geometry of
{\rm Spec}(A)
and the properties of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
in the noncommutative case that are at the heart of the discussion in Section
7
. We also notice, that instead, if we work with algebras over
A
, the base change theorem holds,
i.e.
it is true that

D\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong D\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B

for any
D\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{C}
. And this property is what we used to prove Proposition
4.4
.

We will see in Section
5
that the connection between the geometry of
{\rm Spec}(A)
and the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is not lost in the noncommutative case as homotopical epimorphisms are precisely determined by specific localizations of the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
.

4.2  The category of localizations

We now want to associate to any object
R\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
a geometric space using the homotopy Zariski topology on
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. The procedure we adopt here is the same that can be used to obtain the Grothendieck spectrum of a commutative ring from the data of the classical Zariski topology on the category of affine schemes
8
8

8

In Proposition
5.10
we will give more details about this procedure in the classical setting.
.
To do this we introduce the following notation.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
R\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and let us denote
X={\rm Spec}(R)\in{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

•

We define
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
, the category of
localizations
of
R
, to be the subcategory of all homotopy epimorphisms in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
whose domain is
R
.

•

We define
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
, the category of
open immersions
of
X
, to be the subcategory of all homotopy monomorphisms in
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{R}

whose codomain is
X
.

Observe that, by definition, we have
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)={\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)^{\rm op\,}
.
Before moving forward with the study of the categories
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
and
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
, let us discuss some examples of formal homotopy Zariski localizations.

Example 4.8
.

(i)

Classical Zariski localizations.
Let
R
be a (discrete) commutative ring and consider a
Zariski localization
, namely a morphism of commutative rings
R\to S
that induces an open embedding
{\rm Spec}(S)\to{\rm Spec}(R)
for the classical Zariski topology. As shown in
[
60
, Lemma 2.1.4 (1)]
, a Zariski localization induces a homotopical epimorphism in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. For the sake of completeness, let us mention that the family of Zariski localizations can be also characterised algebraically as the family of flat epimorphisms of finite presentation. Notice also that
[
60
, Lemma 2.1.4]
shows that the homotopy Zariski topology on commutative simplicial rings precisely restricts to the classical Zariski topology on discrete commutative rings. In this sense, the homotopical Zariski topology is a generalization of the classical Zariski topology of scheme theory.

(ii)

Flat epimorphisms.
A generalisation of the previous example is the class of flat epimorphisms between (discrete) rings
f:R\to S
. This class encompasses the class of Zariski localizations and although it is
a priori
a huge class, it is well-known that the family of isomorphism classes (in the category of
R
-algebras) of epimorphisms with fixed source ring
R
is a set. The sub-class of flat ring epimorphisms has been thoroughly studied in the literature and, by definition, is a sub-class of homotopical epimorphisms of ring. Indeed, if
S
is flat over
R
, then the canonical map
S\otimes_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}S\to S\otimes_{R}S
is an equivalence; since
f
is an epimorphism,
S\otimes_{R}S\to S
is an isomorphism, and the fact that the composition
S\otimes_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}S\to S\otimes_{R}S\to S
is an equivalence is precisely the request of
R\to S
being a homotopical epimorphism.

The class of flat ring epimorphism is a nice and easy-to-study class of morphisms — a natural candidate for a noncommutative generalisation of the class of Zariski localizations. However, at closer inspection one realises that this class cannot be used in the definition of a Grothendieck topology for noncommutative rings, because it is not stable under push-outs of rings. To see this, consider the following situation. Let
R
be a ring and
s\in R
be such that the multiplicative subset
\{1,s,s^{2},\ldots\}\subset R
is not an Ore subset and the localization
R\to R[s^{-1}]
is not flat. Note that such examples exist (see,
e.g.
, Example (vi) below). Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}[x]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}[x,x^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[s^{-1}]}

is a cocartesian diagram of rings (
i.e.
,
R[s^{-1}]\cong R\ast_{{\mathbb{Z}}[x]}{\mathbb{Z}}[x,x^{-1}]
), where the left vertical map is given by
x\mapsto s
. But
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]\to{\mathbb{Z}}[x,x^{-1}]
is the prototype of a flat morphism of commutative rings, whereas
R\to R[s^{-1}]
is by construction not flat.

(iii)

Ore localizations.
If
S\subset R
is a multiplicative subset that satisfies the Ore condition
9
9

9

The (right) Ore condition for a multiplicative subset
S
of a ring
R
is that for
a\in R
and
s\in S
, the intersection
aS\cap sR\neq\emptyset
.
, then the localization map
R\to R[S^{-1}]
is a flat ring epimorphism, and therefore it is a homotopical epimorphism. If
S
does not satisfy the Ore conditions, then the localization map
R\to R[S^{-1}]
is not flat and in general is not even a homotopical epimorphism.

(iv)

Localizations of discrete commutative rings.
If
A
is a commutative simplicial ring (
i.e.
,
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
) and
A\to B
a homotopical epimorphism then
B
is commutative (
i.e.
,
B\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
)
10
10

10

An easy way to prove this assertion is the following. It is known, and we will give a proof in Proposition
5.13
, that any homotopy epimorphism
A\to B
determines a unique smashing localization of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
. In this case, the smashing subcategory generated by
A
, that is the unit of the
\otimes
-triangulated structure in
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
, is
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
itself and it follows that all smashing subcategories,
i.e.
kernels of smashing localizations, are
\otimes
-ideals. It is then a standard consequence of the Eckmann–Hilton argument that the algebra constructed in Proposition
5.13
is commutative in this situation.
. Moreover, if
A
is Noetherian and discrete, then
B
is also discrete by
[
2
, Theorem 1.1 (2)]
. In the next example we briefly discuss the non-Noetherian case, so let us focus on the Noetherian case here. In this situation, by the main results of
[
46
]
, localizing subcategories of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
precisely correspond to subsets of prime ideals spectrum
{\rm Spec}(A)
and so do in particular smashing subcategories. Therefore, the smashing localizations of
D^{\leq 0}({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
correspond to inclusions
{\rm Spec}(B)\to{\rm Spec}(A)
where the image is a generalization closed subset.
This property of homotopical localizations of discrete rings being concentrated in degree
0
crucially fails for noncommutative rings because of the existence of non-flat localizations. The Example (vi) illustrates an explicit instance where this phenomenon occurs.

(v)

Localizations of non-Noetherian valuation rings.
In the case of non-Noetherian commutative rings, the situation is more complicated than in the Noetherian case. Still, at least in some case, the family of homotopy epimorphisms can be explicitly described. In
[
14
, Section 2]
there is a summary of the classification of homotopical epimorphisms for valuation rings
R
. For example, in this situation, if
R
is a valuation ring with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
such that
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}^{2}
, then it is possible to show that the (non-flat) homomorphism
R\to\frac{R}{\mathfrak{m}}
is a homotopical epimoprhism. A similar phenomenon happens in the theory of commutative
C^{*}
-algebras as shown in
[
10
]
.

(vi)

Localizations of discrete noncommutative rings.
We now give an example of a homotopical epimorphism from a discrete noncommutative ring to a non-discrete simplicial ring. This example is taken from
[
20
, Example 5.5]
and adapted to the conventions used in this paper. Let us work with connective dg-algebras and tacitly use the Dold–Kan equivalence whenever needed. Let
R
be a commutative ring that, for simplicity, we assume to be of characteristic zero. Consider the
R
-algebra

A=\frac{R\langle s,t,u\rangle}{(st,us)}.

One can show that the multiplicative subset
\{1,s,s^{2},s^{3},\ldots\}\subset A
does not satisfy the Ore conditions and therefore the localization
A\to A[s^{-1}]
is not flat. Moreover, one can check that it is not true that
A[s^{-1}]\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}A[s^{-1}]\cong A[s^{-1}]
and therefore this localization is not a homotopical epimorphism. This failure can be corrected by considering the homotopical localization of
A
at
s^{-1}
, obtained, roughly speaking, by inverting
s
homotopically rather than in the category of rings. We refer to
[
20
]
for more details about this procedure of homotopical inversion of elements of dg-algebras in general. For the purpose of this example it is enough to know that the result of this operation is always a homotopical epimorphism but the target ring might not be discrete even if the domain is. In our situation we can define the localization of
A
at
s
via the free product

A\ast_{R[s]}^{{\mathbb{L}}}R[s,s^{-1}],

that is meant to be the push-out of the diagram

\hbox to152.65pt{\vbox to61.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 76.32251pt\lower-30.97061pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{
