{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{35.96599pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 49.77 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the map
R[x]\to A
is defined by
x\mapsto s
. Notice that
R[x]\to R[x,x^{-1}]
is one of the most basic Zariski localizations of algebraic geometry, and hence it is a homotopical epimorphism by Example (i). And by Proposition
4.4
this implies that
A\to A\ast_{R[s]}^{{\mathbb{L}}}R[s,s^{-1}]
is a homotopical epimorphism. To explicitly compute the derived free product, we can follow
[
20
, Example 5.5]
where it is shown that the cofibrant
R
-dg-algebra

C=R\langle s,t,u,z,w,a\rangle

is a
R[s]-
cofibrant replacement of
A
.
Here
z
and
w
are generators in degree
11
11

11

We should warn the reader that in
[
20
, Example 5.5]
the degrees are positive, because
[
20
]
adopts homological differentials for dg-modules, rather than cohomological differentials used in the present paper.

-1
,
a
is a generator in degree
-2
, and the differentials are defined by
dz=st
,
dw=us
and
da=uz-wt
. Therefore, a calculation gives us

A\ast_{R[s]}^{{\mathbb{L}}}R[s,s^{-1}]\cong C\ast_{R[s]}R[s,s^{-1}].

One can check that the latter dg-algebra has cohomology in all even negative degrees (see
[
20
, Example 5.5]
for a detailed computation).

With the next proposition, we shall show that the category
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
is equivalent to a complete join semi-lattice, namely, a partially ordered set that admits a least upper bound (join) for any nonempty arbitrary collection of subsets.

Proposition 4.9
.

The category
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
is essentially small and is equivalent to a complete join semi-lattice.

Proof.

To prove that
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
is essentially small we notice that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a homotopically well-copowered category. One way see why this is true
12
12

12

Another possibility is to notice that well-copoweredness is always true for an algebraic category and also its homotopical version is true for homotopical algebraic categories. From our description of the category
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
as an algebraic category the reader can imagine that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is a homotopical algebraic category. Since we have not introduced the homotopical version of the notion of algebraic category we prefer to argue in a more down-to-earth way to prove well-copoweredness.
, is to observe that Proposition
4.2
implies that the datum of a homotopy epimorphism is equivalent to a smashing localization of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
(see subsection
5.2
for further details). These in turn are particular cases of smashing localizations of the unbounded derived category
D(R)
and form a set, because
D(R)
is compactly generated (
cf.
the introduction of
[
37
]
). This shows that
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
is essentially small. To show that it is equivalent to a poset, we observe that for any
A,A^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
the set

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}}(A,A^{\prime})

is either empty or a singleton, because the structure maps
R\to A
and
R\to A^{\prime}
are homotopical epimorphisms. This poset has all joins that are given by the derived free product of algebras because it is the homotopical coproduct of algebras.
∎

By duality, the above proposition tells us that the category
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
is equivalent to a complete meet semi-lattice, namely, a partially ordered set that admits a greatest lower bound (meet) for any nonempty arbitrary subset. We will interpret the objects of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
as open subsets of
X={\rm Spec}(R)
, their meets as intersections of open subsets. We do not interpret joins of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
as unions in
X
, because unions will be prescribed by the homotopy Zariski topology and the latter is not always compatible with the lattice structure of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
. There are two main reasons for why the joins in
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
do not precisely correspond to geometric unions in
X
. One is a phenomenon proper of noncommutative geometry that will be presented later on. The other is more classical, it is explained in the next remark and boils down to the fact that the objects of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
form just a base for a topology not all the opens subsets of a topological space.

Remark 4.10
.

Already in the classical setting of the Zariski topology for commutative rings, it can happen that given two
U,V\in{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
, their join in
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
does not agree with their geometric union (this is equivalent to say that the union of two open affine subschemes should be computed in the category of schemes and not in the category of affine schemes). A simple example of this phenomenon happens when
X={\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}}[x,y])
,
U={\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}}[x,y][x^{-1}])
, and
V={\rm Spec}({\mathbb{C}}[x,y][y^{-1}])
. In this case
U\cup V
is not an affine scheme (in the usual sense) and therefore it is not equivalent to
{\rm Spec}(A)
for any
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. But clearly
U\cup V
makes sense as a (non-affine) scheme. Alternatively, one can think of the scheme
U\cup V
as an affine stacks in the sense of Toën (see
[
58
]
) or even better in the framework of complicial algebraic geometry of
[
59
, Chapter 2.3]
. This amounts to work with the whole category of unbounded dg-algebras in place of the category of connective dg-algebras, which coincidentally is the setting of
[
20
]
and
[
23
]
. In this alternative setting, we would get that the construction of
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
and
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
gives the (distributive if
R
is commutative) lattice of smashing localizations of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{R})
into which the
{\mathbf{Loc}}(R)
canonically embeds. But this difference is negligible because the sites associated to these two versions of the constructions are equivalent (see
[
46
]
for more information on the commutative side of this). We prefer to work with connective dg-algebras because we would like to distinguish the cohomological and the infinitesimal directions of derived geometry.

Building upon Remark
4.10
, it is easy to find homotopy epimorphisms from a discrete ring (even a commutative and Noetherian one) to a dg-algebra whose cohomology is in positive degrees (or equivalently to cosimplicial rings, via the cosimplicial version or the stable version of the Dold–Kan equivalence). In classical algebraic geometry, such examples correspond to non-affine sub-schemes of affine schemes, and their dg-algebras correspond to their sheaf cohomology dg-algebra and are examples of affine stacks in the sense of Toën (see
[
58
]
and
[
59
, Chapter 2.3]
for more on the notion of affine stacks).
The fact that non-affine schemes must be detected by their cohomology is an immediate consequence of Serre’s criterion for affineness. This observation says nothing new about the geometry of affine schemes, it just underlines that the geometry of affine schemes is interesting to study. A fact that does not require derived geometry to be appreciated. Therefore, we omit to discuss the “complicial” version of the theory in this work (
i.e.
using unbounded dg-algebras and modules), and focus on what we believe to be the more interesting connective part of the theory, from which the complicial theory follows formally by stabilisation.

4.3  The noncommutative spectrum

By restricting the formal homotopy Zariski topology, that is defined on
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, to the subcategory
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
, with
X={\rm Spec}(R)
, we are finally in a position to introduce the small homotopy Zariski site. Notice that for all
X
we have that
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
is stable with respect to the homotopy Zariski topology in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
,
i.e.
it is stable by base changes and by homotopy Zariski covers.

Definition 4.11
.

For
X\in{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we define
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
to be the small site whose underlying category is
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
and covers are homotopical Zariski covers.

Since
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
is stable under pullbacks and covers in
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, then Definition
4.11
is well posed. The association

(4.2)

X\mapsto{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}

immediately warrants the following questions.

•

Does the site
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
have enough points?

•

Is the association (
4.2
) functorial?

•

Is
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
equivalent
13
13

13

We recall that two sites
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are called equivalent if there is a morphism of sites
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
that induces an equivalence
{\mathbf{Sh}}({\mathbf{D}})\to{\mathbf{Sh}}({\mathbf{C}})
between the categories of sheaves. We underline that sites that a priori look very different can be equivalent.
to the site of a sober topological space?

In the remainder of this subsection, we will address these questions in turn, and answer each of them in the positive.

Proposition 4.12
.

The site
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
has enough points and is equivalent to the site of a sober topological space.

Proof.

The topology of
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
is defined by families of covers whose cardinality is finite; therefore, it is a coherent site. By Deligne’s Theorem, coherent sites have enough points (see
[
43
, Corollary IX.11.3]
). Then, by
[
43
, Theorem IX.5.1]
a topos is localic (
i.e.
, equivalent to the topos of a locale) if and only if can be defined on a poset. Finally, by
[
43
, Proposition IX.3.3]
a localic topos is equivalent to the topos of a topological space if and only if it has enough points.
∎

A description of the points of the topos
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
will be provided later in Proposition
4.17
.

Proposition 4.13
.

The association (
4.2
) is a 2-1 pseudofunctor
14
14

14

Since the class of all sites forms a
2
-category, not just an ordinary category, the correct notion of “morphism” from a category to a
2
-category is that of a 2-1 pseudofunctor. This is a minor technical detail that disappears when working with
\infty
-categories, which is necessary because notions in category theory are defined up to equivalence and not up to isomorphism. It is possible to avoid the use of
2-1
pseudofunctors by passing to equivalences classes and “strictifying” 2-1 pseudofunctors to actual functors. The result of this process is not very natural, hence we refrain from doing this. The reader unfamiliar with the notion of 2-1 pseudofunctors and
2
-categories can ignore this nuance and just think in terms of functors: this usually does not lead to mistakes.

{\mathbf{dAff}}_{R}\to{\mathbf{Sites}}
, where
{\mathbf{Sites}}
is the 2-category of small sites whose morphisms are continuous functors
15
15

15

These are functors that preserve cover families and are cover-flat.
.

Proof.

Let
f:B\to A
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
{\rm Spec}(A)=Y\xrightarrow{f^{{\rm op\,}}}X={\rm Spec}(B)
the corresponding morphism in
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. We define the functor
\widetilde{f}:{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}\to{\mathbf{Zar}}_{Y}\qquad
as

(U\to X)\mapsto(Y\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}U\to Y)\,.

We first observe that this construction gives a 2-1 pseudofunctor rather than a functor. Indeed, if we consider another morphism
Y\xrightarrow{g^{{\rm op\,}}}Z={\rm Spec}(C)
, then we have a canonical natural isomorphism of functors

\widetilde{g\circ f}\cong\widetilde{g}\circ\widetilde{f}

instead of an equality. This is due to the fact that the canonical isomorphism

Z\times_{Y}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}U)\cong Z\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}U

is not the identity in general.

We need to check that the functor
\widetilde{f}
is well defined and preserves covers for the homotopy Zariski topology. This is equivalent to show that the functor

f^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}

defined by
f^{*}(C):=C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B
preserves homotopy epimorphisms and homotopical Zariski covers. For the first property, we retrace the arguments from the proof of Proposition
4.4
, where a similar statement was proved for a morphism
A\to B
that is a homotopy epimorphism, observing that the latter property did not play any role in the proof. As to the second property, let us consider a homotopical Zariski cover
\{U_{i}\to X\}
. We first notice that the morphisms of the family
\{U_{i}\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}Y\to Y\}
are homotopical Zariski open embeddings because the functor
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B
preserves homotopy epimorphisms. Then, the fact that
\{U_{i}\times_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}Y\to Y\}
is a cover follows using the homotopical pullback lemma, as in Proposition
4.4
.

It remains to show that
\widetilde{f}
is cover-flat,
i.e.
, that
\widetilde{f}
commutes with finite homotopy limits.
To this end, it is enough to show that for any pair of maps
U\to V
and
U\to W
in
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
we have that
\widetilde{f}(V\times_{U}^{\mathbb{R}}W)=\widetilde{f}(V)\times_{\widetilde{f}(U)}^{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{f}(W)
. To do this computation, we switch to the opposite category. By putting
U={\rm Spec}(C)
,
V={\rm Spec}(D)
and
W={\rm Spec}(E)
, we get that the homotopy push-out square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{C}E}

is mapped to the homotopy push-out square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{C}E*^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}B}

because the derived free product preserves homotopy push-outs. Alternatively, one can check that there is a canonical chain of isomorphisms

D\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}E\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\simeq D\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\ast_{C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B}^{\mathbb{L}}E\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\simeq(D\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)\ast_{C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B}^{\mathbb{L}}(E\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)

where we use the canonical isomorphism of functors

D\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{C}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\cong D\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\ast_{C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})

and

D\ast^{\mathbb{L}}_{C}C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\cong D\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$}).

∎

Motivated by the previous results, we are finally in the position to define the noncommutative spectrum for a noncommutative ring.

Definition 4.14
.

For any
R\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we call
noncommutative spectrum

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
the sober topological space equivalent to
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
given by Proposition
4.12
.

Observe that the space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
is defined up to homeomorphism.

Corollary 4.15
.

The noncommutative spectrum

(4.3)

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbf{Top}}\,,

is a functor from the homotopy category of connective dg-algebras over
{\mathbb{Z}}
to the category of topological spaces.

Proof.

The association
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}\mapsto{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
is a functor from the 2-category of sites to the category of topological spaces. The pre-composition with the 2-1 pseudofunctor described in Proposition
4.13
yields the functor (
4.3
).
∎

4.4  The notion of point in the noncommutative spectrum

In Proposition
4.12
we have used abstract results from topos theory and the theory of locales to show that the topos associated to
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
has enough points. We now give a more concrete description of the points of the topological space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
. In preparation for this, let us recall the following definition from topos theory.

Definition 4.16
.

Let
(S,\tau)
be a poset equipped with a topology. An
ideal
of
(S,\tau)
is a subset
I\subset S
such that

(i)

given
s,t\in S
, the conditions
s\leq t
and
t\in I
imply
s\in I
;

(ii)

if
\{s_{i}\to s\}
is a cover and
s_{i}\in I
for all
i
, then
s\in I
.

We denote the set of all ideals of
(S,\tau)
by
{\rm Id\,}((S,\tau))
.

Observe that, when
S
is a distributive lattice and
\tau
is the family of covers given by finite joins, it is well known that
{\rm Id\,}((S,\tau))
is the frame of open subsets of a spectral topological space whose lattice of compact open subsets is isomorphic to
S
. Furthermore, all spectral topological spaces can be written as the frame of ideals of some distributive lattice.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
X={\rm Spec}(A)
. Then
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is the sober topological space associated with the frame of ideals
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})
. Therefore, points of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
correspond to completely prime ultrafilters of
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})
.

Proof.

Given a poset with a topology
(S,\tau)
, the poset
{\rm Id\,}((S,\tau))
with respect to the inclusion of sets is a frame (see
[
36
, Proposition II.2.11]
). The fact that
(S,\tau)
and
{\rm Id\,}((S,\tau))
are equivalent sites is the content of
[
43
, Exercise IX.5 (c)]
. Finally, the last claim is a straightforward application of the definition of points of a locale to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
(see, e.g.,
[
43
, Chapter IX]
and
[
36
, Chapter II]
for the basics of the theory of locales).
∎

The poset
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X}
and its topology contain all the information required to construct the topological space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
(in concrete terms, it contains a base for the topology). In particular, the frame
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})
is the frame of open subsets of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
.
A point
x\in{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
belongs to an open subset
U\subset{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
corresponding to an element
{\mathcal{U}}\in{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})
if and only if
{\mathcal{U}}\in{\mathscr{I}}_{x}
, where
{\mathscr{I}}_{x}
is the completely prime filter associated to
x
. This correspondence maps objects of
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})
to subsets of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
, thus yielding an injective map
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})\to{\mathscr{P}}({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A))
that intertwines the partial order in
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{X})
with the inclusion order in
{\mathscr{P}}({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A))
. This map commutes with arbitrary unions and finite intersections, but does not commute with infinite intersections in general (because the intersection of an infinite family of open subsets is not an open subset in general).

5  Properties of the noncommutative spectrum

The aim of this section is twofold:

1.

To construct a map from the noncommutative spectrum of a commutative ring to its Grothendieck spectrum of prime ideals;

2.

To recast our definition of the spectrum in terms of smashing subcategories of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
.

5.1  Comparison with the Grothendieck spectrum

Our definition of (formal) Zariski covers differs from the one commonly used in derived algebraic geometry in a key aspect. In derived algebraic geometry one requires the pullback functors
\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}\}_{i}
to be conservative. If Definition
4.3
is reinterpreted in the category of commutative (homotopical) rings, then one recovers the usual notion of cover, because for commutative (homotopical) rings the base change of algebras essentially agrees with the base change of modules. However, in the noncommutative setting the base changes of algebras is given by the free product, which usually does not agree with the base change of the underlying module.

This raises the issue of comparing the new notion of cover with its commonly used counterpart. Before discussing this matter further, let us show by a simple counterexample that the two notions are, indeed, not equivalent for commutative rings.

Example 5.1
.

Let
k
be a commutative field. In the classical setting, the Grothendieck spectrum of
k\oplus k
is nothing but
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)=\{\star,\star\}
and a cover is given by the two projections
\pi_{1}:k\oplus k\to k
and
\pi_{2}:k\oplus k\to k
. However, this is not the case in our setting, since the family of homotopical epimorphisms
\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\}
is not a cover in the sense of Definition
4.3
. Indeed, consider the diagonal embedding
k\oplus k\to M_{2}(k)
into the algebra of
2\times 2
square matrices. Then, it is easy to check that
k\ast_{k\oplus k}^{\mathbb{L}}M_{2}(k)\cong 0
when computed via both maps
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
. But
M_{2}(k)\neq 0
, therefore the family
\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\}
is not conservative in the sense of the definition
16
16

16

We would like to thank Ryo Kanda for drawing our attention to this example.
.

In order to compare our new notion of cover with the classical one, we will resort to the following.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. A finite family of homotopical epimophisms
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i}
is conservative if and only if for
C\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
it holds that

C\cong 0\iff C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\cong 0\qquad\text{for all $i$.}

Proof.

Suppose that there exists
B\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
such that
C\neq 0
and
C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}=0
for all
i
. Then, the canonical morphism
C\to 0
is sent to an isomorphism by all functors
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}
, but it is not an isomorphism. Thus, we do not have a conservative family of functors.

Conversely, if
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i}
is not conservative there exists a morphism
C\to D
in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
that is not an isomorphism such that
C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\to D\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}
is an isomorphism for all
i
. Taking the cofibers of the last morphisms gives
0
, so
{\rm cofib\,}(C\to D)
is a non-zero object of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
such that
{\rm cofib\,}(C\to D)\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}=0
for all
i
, because derived functors commute with cofibers.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\{f_{i}:A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a cover for the homotopy Zariski topology. Then the family of functors

\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}\}_{i\in I}

is faithful.

Proof.

Suppose that
f:B\to C
is a morphism in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
such that
f\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\cong 0
for all
i
. Then, since the algebras in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B_{i}}
are unital algebras and morphisms are morphisms of unital algebras, this implies that
C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\cong 0
for all
i\in I
. Applying Lemma
5.2
we find that
C\cong 0
, and hence
f=0
.
We then notice that for any
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
we can embed the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
faithfully into the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}
noticing that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}={\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}A^{\rm op\,}}
and use the base change via the canonical morphism of rings
A\to A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}A^{\rm op\,}
given by
a\mapsto a\otimes a^{\rm op\,}
. It is easy to check that the obtained base change functor is faithful.

Then, we can faithfully embed the category of bimodules over
A
into
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A_{i}}
by means of the free algebra functor
{\mathbf{Free}}_{A}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
. Notice that if
A\to B
is a homotopical epimorphism, then for all
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}
we have

{\mathbf{Free}}_{A}(M)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong{\mathbf{Free}}_{B}(M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B).

Indeed,

{\mathbf{Free}}_{A}(M)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong(A\oplus M\oplus M^{(\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}})^{2}}\oplus\cdots)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong

(B\oplus M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\oplus(M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)^{(\otimes_{B}^{\mathbb{L}})^{2}}\oplus\cdots)\cong{\mathbf{Free}}_{B}(M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)

because the relation
B\cong B\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B
can be used iteratively. We also observe that there is a natural isomorphism

{\mathbf{Free}}_{A}(M)\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B\cong{\mathbf{Free}}_{B}(M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B)

because both algebras satisfy the same universal property. Indeed, let
C\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}
, then by working out definitions we get

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}}({\mathbf{Free}}_{B}(M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B),C)\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{B}}(M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B,C)\cong

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}}(M,C)\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}}({\mathbf{Free}}_{A}(M),C)\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B}}({\mathbf{Free}}_{A}(M)\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B,C).

This shows that there is a commutative (up to natural isomorphism) square of functors

\hbox to286.94pt{\vbox to64.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 143.46599pt\lower-30.53374pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We can put such squares together for the family of homotopical epimorphisms
A\to B_{i}
in the statement of the proposition and thus obtain a commutative square of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{BiMod}}_{B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i\in I}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B_{i}}}

where the horizontal functors are faithful. This fact plus the computation in the beginning of the proof show that the only morphism in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
that is sent to
0
by the composition of the functors is the zero morphism. Since the bottom horizontal maps are faithful, the same is true also for remaining vertical maps. But this is equivalent to saying that the map is faithful because the left vertical functor is an additive functor.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Note that Example
5.1
shows that, unlike in the classical situation, in the noncommutative setting there exist faithful families of localization functors
\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
for
f_{i}
homotopical epimorphisms that are not covers in the sense of Definition
4.3
.

Thus, even though — as per Remark
5.4
—- the converse to Proposition
5.3
does not hold, this is enough to compare
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
to the classical
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
when
A
is a classical commutative ring. To do this we introduce an intermediate topology.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. We define the
fine (homotopical) Zariski topology
on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
to be the topology whose covers are finite families of homotopical epimorphisms
\{B\to C_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that for all homotopical epimorphisms
B\to D
we have
D\cong 0
if and only if
D_{i}\ast_{B}^{\mathbb{L}}C_{i}\cong 0
for all
i\in I
.

Remark 5.6
.

Observe that by Lemma
5.2
, the condition of cover from Definition
5.5
is the same as that in Definition
4.3
, but restricted only to the subcategory of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}
of homotopical epimorphisms out of
A
.

Proposition 5.7
.

The fine Zariski topology is well defined and endows
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
with the structure of a coherent posite, that is therefore equivalent to a topological space which we denote
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.4
. Let
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a cover for the fine Zariski topology, and let
A\to C
be homotopical epimorphism. We need to show that the family
\{C\to C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover as well. Let
C\to D
be a homotopical epimorphism such that
C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong 0
for all
i
, then we have

C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong B_{i}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}C\ast_{C}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong B_{i}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}D\,.

Since the composition
A\to C\to D
is a homotopical epimorphism, one can conclude that
D\cong 0
, and, hence, that
\{C\to C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover.

It remains only to check that if
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover and for every
i\in I
we have covers
\{B_{i}\to C_{j}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
, then the composition
\{A\to C_{j}\}_{j\in\cup_{i\in I}J_{i}}
is a cover. Let
A\to D
be a homotopical epimorphism such that
A\ast_{C_{j}}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong 0
for all
j\in\bigcup_{i\in I}J_{i}
. Then, by the isomorphism

A\ast_{C_{j}}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong A\ast_{B_{i}}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\ast_{C_{j}}^{\mathbb{L}}D

we deduce that
B_{i}\ast_{C_{j}}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong 0
for all
j
. Since the families
\{B_{i}\to C_{j}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
are covers, it ensues that
B_{i}\ast_{C_{j}}^{\mathbb{L}}D\cong 0
for all
i\in I
. The latter, in turn, implies
D\cong 0
because
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Let us emphasise that the fine Zariski topology is not functorial in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Indeed, the map
k\oplus k\to M_{2}(k)
from Example
5.1
induces a functor
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(k\oplus k)\to{\mathbf{Ouv}}(M_{2}(k))
given by
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\ast_{k\oplus k}M_{2}(k)
which sends the cover (for the fine Zariski topology)
\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\}
to the family of maps
\{M_{2}(k)\to 0,M_{2}(k)\to 0\}
. But the latter is not a cover for the fine Zariski topology. It follows that the fine Zariski topology is functorial when restricted to the subcategory of homotopical epimorphisms.
We will elaborate further on the geometry of this situation in Example
6.1
.

In the light of Proposition
5.7
, we are now in a position to compare the classical spectrum of a commutative ring
A
to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
via
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
by means of the following lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

1.

Localizations for the classical Zariski topology are homotopical epimorphism.

2.

A family of classical localizations forms a cover for the classical Zariski topology if and only if it forms a cover for the fine Zariski topology.

Proof.

1.

This is a well known fact, whose proof we outline below. Let
f:A\to B
be a homotopical epimorphism
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
that is of finite presentation (in the commutative sense). Then one can show that
B
must be concentrated in degree
0
(
cf.

[
59
, Proposition 2.2.2.5 (4)]
) and that
f
is a flat epimorphism of commutative rings (
cf.

[
60
, Lemma 2.1.4 (i)]
). Therefore, the class of homotopical epimorphisms of finite presentation under
A
precisely corresponds to the class of localizations for the classical Zariski topology on
A
.

2.

Consider a cover
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for the classical Zariski topology. One can show (
cf.

[
60
, Lemma 2.1.4 (ii)]
) that this is equivalent to require the derived descent condition for the category of modules (
i.e.
by the conservativity of the family of functors
(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}
). But this is, in turn, equivalent to the condition of being a cover for the fine Zariski topology, because the latter is equivalent to ask that the objects
B_{i}
generate
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
as a pre-triangulated category.

∎

At this poing, we have all tools at our disposal to compare the noncommutative spectrum with the classical notion of spectrum of prime ideals of a commutative ring introduced by Grothendieck, which, for the sake of clarity, we denote by
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, there exist canonical maps of topological spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-37.41829pt}{-1.00694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -51.78 -1.39)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.9
, the classical Zariski topology admits a homotopical characterisation, which implies that the posite that determines
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is canonically a sub-posite of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
with the fine Zariski topology. Moreover, the inclusion functor identifies the restriction of the fine Zariski topology to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
with the classical Zariski topology. This gives the projection map
\pi_{A}
. The map
\iota_{A}
is obtained by observing that Proposition
5.3
implies that the identity functor
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)\to{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
is continuous when the domain is endowed with the homotopy Zariski topology and the codomain with the fine Zariski topology.
∎

The map
\pi_{A}
defines a projection from
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
. We expect this map to be surjective and even a quotient map, at least in favourable cases.

We conclude the subsection with a final remark on the functoriality of the fine Zariski topology. For any
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}
, one always has the canonical map
\iota_{A}:{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
, Defined as in the proof of Proposition
5.10
. Thus, although
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
is not functorial in
A
, for any morphism
B\to A
in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}
we have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)}
\iota_{A}
\iota_{B}

where the dashed arrow does not always exists as a continuous map of topological spaces but always exists as a continuous correspondence

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)\leftarrow{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(B).

This is reminiscent of other constructions of spectra of rings previously appeared in literature:
cf.

[
3
, Section 4]
. Therefore, albeit in a weaker sense, the fine Zariski topology has functorial properties. We will see in Example
6.1
one such correspondence that is not a function.

5.2  Comparison with the spectrum of smashing subcategories

In order to perform explicit computations of the topological space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
, let us give an alternative characterisation of homotopy epimorphisms. This will reduce the problem of describing
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
to the problem of understanding the lattice of some specific kind of subcategories of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
that we now specify.
Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and consider a full embedding of categories
i_{*}:{\mathbf{C}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
such that

(i)

the functor
i_{*}
is a triangulated functor,
i.e.
, it commutes with the shift operation;

(ii)

the category
{\mathbf{C}}
is closed by finite coproducts, direct summands and cones;

(iii)

the functor
i_{*}
has both a left and a right adjoint, denoted
i^{*}
and
i^{!}
, respectively.

Definition 5.11
.

We call
smashing localization
of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
the functor
i^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
smashing subcategory
of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
the kernel of
i^{*}
.

Remark 5.12
.

Observe that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is not triangulated because the suspension functor is not an equivalence. This kind of category is sometimes called
pre-triangulated
category. By stabilizing
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
we obtain a triangulated category that is precisely
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
, the unbounded derived category of
A
-modules. It is easy to check that the notion of smashing localization introduced in Definition
5.11
is compatible with the standard notion of smashing localization from the theory of triangulated categories. Therefore, Definition
5.11
determines a subclass of the smashing localizations of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
.

The next proposition already appears in literature in several different versions. We refer to
[
47
, §4]
for one of the earliest appearances. We provide a full proof for the version that is more suitable to our setting.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.
Smashing localizations of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
and homotopy epimorphisms of
A
to connective dg-algebras are in 1-to-1 correspondence.

Proof.

Let
A\to B
be a homotopical epimorphism. Then the inclusion of the full subcategory
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is a smashing localization because the restriction of scalars functor always has both adjoints.

Let
i^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{C}}
be a smashing localization. Since

i_{*}
is fully faithful, we have that

{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Hom\,}_{\mathbf{C}}(i^{*}(A),i^{*}(A))\cong{\mathbb{R}}{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}}(A,i_{*}i^{*}(A))\cong i^{*}(A)

equips
i^{*}(A)
with a structure of dg-algebra for which the canonical map
A\to i^{*}(A)
is a morphism of dg-algebras. Then, since
i^{*}
is a left adjoint functor for
i_{*}
, we have that

i^{*}(M)\cong i^{*}(A)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}M

for all
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
, because the functors
i^{*}
and
i^{*}(A)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
have the same values on the generators of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
17
17

17

It is well-known that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is generated by
A
, see for instance
[
55
, Theorem 3.1.1]
for a much stronger result.
and both commute with direct sums and cones. But since
i^{*}
is a localization, the functorial isomorphism

i^{*}\circ i^{*}\cong i^{*}

translates, via the functor
i^{*}(A)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
, into
the functorial isomorphism

i^{*}(A)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\circ i^{*}(A)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\cong i^{*}(A)\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}(\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})\,.

This is equivalent to say that
A
is a homotopical epimorphism.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The same argument as in Proposition
5.13
shows that there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between smashing localizations of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
and homotopy epimorphisms of
A
to (unbounded) dg-algebras. From our perspective, dg-algebras with cohomology in positive degrees correspond to non-affine objects which can be viewed as affine stacks in the sense of Toën. Thus, in the setting of complicial algebraic geometry these are still affine objects determined by their dg-algebra of functions. We also mention that a similar setting has been recently considered in
[
49
]
where such algebras are called
derived algebras
.

Definition 5.15
.

We denote by
{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
the poset of smashing subcategories of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
.

Proposition 5.16
.

The bijection established by Proposition
5.13
yields an isomorphism of posets

{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})\cong{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)\,,

where
X={\rm Spec}(A)
.

Proof.

We only need to check that the bijection in Proposition
5.13
preserves the partial orders. Let
f:A\to B
be a homotopy epimorphism and
{\mathbb{L}}f^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}
be the corresponding localization. The partial order in
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
is given by the reversing the inclusion of the localizations
f_{*}
with
f
ranging in the set of homotopy epimorphisms with domain
A
. This is isomorphic (by Proposition
5.13
) to the poset obtained by equipping the kernels of the localizations
{\mathbb{L}}f^{*}
with partial order given by inclusions. The latter poset is, by definition,
{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
.
∎

We showed in Proposition
4.9
that
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
is a complete meet semi-lattice. By
[
36
, Proposition I.4.3]
this implies that
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
is a complete lattice, so that by the isomorphism of Proposition
5.16
we have that meets and joins on
{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
and
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
agree. More precisely, meets in
{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
are given by the intersections of subcategories whereas meets in
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
are the fiber products of homotopical monomorphisms.
Joins in
{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
are given by the smallest smashing subcategory generated by a family of objects therein, whereas joins in
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
are the corresponding localizations.

Proposition
5.16
offers a way to simplify the computation of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
. This happens in particular under additional conditions on
A
.

Corollary 5.17
.

Suppose that
A
is concentrated in degree
0
and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
satisfy the telescope conjecture
18
18

18

The telescope conjecture — see, e.g.,
[
37
]
— has been verified for certain rings (for example, hereditary rings), disproven for others (for instance, non-Noetherian rings), and it is an open question to characterise the class of rings or dg-algebras for which it holds.
. Then

{\mathbf{Sma}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})\cong{\mathbf{Thick}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}^{c})\,,

where
{\mathbf{Thick}}({\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}^{c})
is the lattice of thick subcategories
19
19

19

A subcategory of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is called
thick
if it satisfies the first two conditions in Definition
5.11
.
of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}^{c}
, and
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}^{c}
is the full subcategory of compact objects
20
20

20

An object
C\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
is called
compact
if the functor
{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}}(C,\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$})
commutes with direct sums.
of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
.

Proof.

The telescope conjecture (for a given pre-triangulated category
{\mathbf{C}}
) asserts that every smashing subcategory is determined by its restriction to the category of compact objects, which is precisely the claim.
∎

At this point, one might wonder whether it is possible to characterise the condition of cover for the homotopy Zariski topology in terms of the localization functors of the categories of modules.
Unfortunately, such a characterisation seems precluded, as Example
5.1
shows. Nevertheless, it could still be the case that such a characterisation holds for the fine Zariski topology, as stated in the following conjecture.

Conjecture 5.18
.

If a family of homotopical epimorphisms
\{f_{i}:A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover for the fine Zariski topology then the family of restriction functors

\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}\}_{i\in I}

is faithful.

Evidence for this conjecture is supplied by the examples we can explicitly work out. In the case where
A
is commutative Noetherian ring we can prove that conjecture is true.

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and let
A\to B
and
A\to C
be homotopical epimorphisms. Then we have a canonical isomorphism

B\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}C\cong B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}C.

Proof.

We observe that since
A
is commutative, then both
B
and
C
are commutative.
It is enough to prove that

B\otimes_{A}C\cong B\ast_{A}C

when both
B
and
C
are cofibrant as
A
-algebras. In this case, the structure maps
A\to B
and
A\to C
are split injections of the underlying
A
-modules, and therefore we can write

B=A\oplus\overline{B},\qquad C=A\oplus\overline{C},

where
\overline{B}
and
\overline{C}
are the cokernels of the structure maps. By
[
23
, text after Definition 3.1 and Lemma 4.3]
we have that
B\otimes_{A}\overline{B}=0
, and by
[
23
, Lemma 2.1]
there exists a filtration

B\ast_{A}C=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}F_{n}

with
F_{0}=0,F_{1}=B
, and

F_{2n+1}/F_{2n}\cong B\otimes_{A}(\overline{C}\otimes_{A}\overline{B})^{\otimes_{A}^{n}},

F_{2n+2}/F_{2n+1}\cong B\otimes_{A}(\overline{C}\otimes_{A}\overline{B})^{\otimes_{A}^{n}}\otimes_{A}\overline{C}.

So, since
A
,
B
and
C
are commutative we have that
\overline{C}\otimes_{A}\overline{B}\cong\overline{B}\otimes_{A}\overline{C}
. We thus get

F_{n+1}/F_{n}\cong 0

for all
n>1
. Finally, we get

B\ast_{A}C=B\oplus B\otimes_{A}\overline{C}\cong B\otimes_{A}A\oplus B\otimes_{A}\overline{C}\cong B\otimes_{A}(A\oplus\overline{C})\cong B\otimes_{A}C\,,\qed

which concludes the proof.

Corollary 5.20
.

Let
A
be a commutative Noetherian ring. Then a family of homotopical epimorphisms
\{f_{i}:A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover for the fine Zariski topology if and only if the family of restriction functors

\{{\mathbb{L}}f_{i}^{*}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}\}_{i\in I}

is faithful. For a general commutative ring, the same holds when the homotopical epimorphisms are of finite presentation.

Proof.

By the main results of
[
2
]
, we know that homotopical epimorphisms of
A
correspond to flat epimorphisms of rings (see also Section
6
for more about this). By
[
46
]
, we know that
f_{i}:A\to B_{i}
identify
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B_{i})
with a generalisation closed subset of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
. And a family
\{f_{i}:A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is faithful if and only if it is surjective on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
. Furthermore, the latter is equivalent to ask that for any other homotopical epimorphism
A\to C
we have

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(C)\times_{{\rm Spec}(A)}{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B_{i})\cong 0

for all
i\in I
if and only if
C=0
. But

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(C)\times_{{\rm Spec}(A)}{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B_{i})\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(C\otimes_{A}B_{i})\,.

Since
B_{i}
and
C
are flat over
A
we have

C\otimes_{A}B_{i}\cong C\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}

and by Theorem
5.19
we have

C\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i}\cong C\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{i},

which yields the first claim. The second claim follows from the fact that homotopical epimorphisms of finite presentation are precisely open embeddings for the classical Zariski topology, and again faithfulness is equivalent to being surjective on the spectrum of prime ideals.
∎

5.3  The relative noncommutative spectrum

Motivated by the previous section, a natural question arises:

what happens if we use the homotopy Zariski topology
21
21

21

For the sake of clarity, let us recall that the homotopy Zariski topology differs from the formal homotopy Zariski topology by requiring that the family of open embeddings is formed by homotopical epimorphisms of finite presentation in the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
.
instead of the formal homotopy Zariski topology?

From the point of view of formal logic, nothing prevents us from using the homotopy Zariski topology, and the resulting theory is still well posed. Let us denote by
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm f.p.}
the spectrum functor one would obtained by pursuing this alternative strategy, where
{\rm f.p.}
stands for finite presentation. As it turns out,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm f.p.}
is badly incompatible with classical algebraic geometry in general. Indeed, Example
3.8
shows that, for any commutative ring
R
that is not finitely generated over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, any non-trivial classical Zariski localization
R\to S
is a homotopy epimorphism that is not of finite presentation. So, there is no natural way of comparing
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm f.p.}(R)
with the Grothendieck spectrum
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
, because the open localizations that form the basis of the topology of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
are not in
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm rel\,}(R)
. Therefore, nothing like the morphism
\iota_{A}
of Proposition
5.10
exists in this case.
Let us make this observation more precise.

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring that is not finitely generated over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm f.p.}(R)=\star

is the singleton topological space.

Proof.

Let
f:R\to S

be a homotopy epimorphism whose domain is
R
and which is of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, since
R
is commutative, so is
S
(see Example
4.8
(iii)), and since
R
is discrete, also
S
must be such (because being of finite presentation in the noncommutative sense implies being of finite presentation in the commutative sense, but not the converse). Therefore,
f
is a classical Zariski localization and, by Example
3.8
,
f
is not finitely presented (in the noncommutative sense) unless it is trivial. Hence,
R\cong S
and
f
is an isomorphism.
∎

The above observations are in perfect agreement with the no-go theorem of Reyes
[
50
]
: it is not possible to define a functor from the category
{\mathbf{Rings}}
to the category
\mathbf{Sets}
such that the spectrum of every
nonzero ring is nonempty and that extends the Grothendieck spectrum functor.
The key point in the proof of Proposition
5.21
is that the notion of finite presentation in the category of commutative
R
-algebras is not compatible with the notion of finite presentation in noncommutative setting.
This notwithstanding, if one restricts oneself to finitely generated (central) algebras over a ring
R
, matters improve. Let us explain why this is the case.

As we observed in Section
3.1
, to a given commutative ring
R
one can associate two different categories of algebras under it: the category
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
of all
R
-algebras and the category
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
of central algebras over
R
. The difference between the two categories can be understood very clearly in terms of algebraic theories: the category
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is the algebraic category generated by the class of algebras

\{{\mathbb{Z}}\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}R\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},

whereas
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is the algebraic category generated by the class of algebras

\{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}.

So, in analogy with the homotopy theory of
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
presented so far, one can develop a homotopy theory of
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
without additional obstacles. Moreover, the two theories are compatible; by this we mean that, since

R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\ast_{R}R\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rangle=R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rangle

for all
n
and
m
, the category
{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is closed by pushouts in
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and similarly
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is closed by homotopical pushouts in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Hence, a morphism
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
is a homotopical epimorphism if and only if it is such when regarded as a morphism in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. However, the notion of finite presentation for objects in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
are very different — see Proposition
3.9
.

With the above discussion in mind, we want to define a notion of the noncommutative spectrum
relative
to a fixed base commutative ring
R
. This functor will be constructed arguing as in Section
4
, but this time we will use the homotopy Zariski topology, instead of the formal homotopy Zariski topology. This topology differs from the formal homotopy Zariski topology by requiring that the open embeddings are deterimined by homotopical epimorphism of finite presentation in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. In this way, it is singled out a subposet
{\mathbf{Loc}}_{\rm rel\,}(A)\subset{\mathbf{Loc}}(A)
and a subposet
{\mathbf{Ouv}}_{\rm rel\,}(X)\subset{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)
, for any
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
and
X={\rm Spec}(A)
. Again, to
{\mathbf{Ouv}}_{\rm rel\,}(X)
we can associate a site
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X,{\rm rel\,}}
that for the same reasons as before has enough points and is equivalent to a site of a sober topological space, that we denote
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X,{\rm rel\,}}
.

Definition 5.22
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. We define the
relative noncommutative spectrum
of
A

to be the topological space

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{R}(A)
associated to the site
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{X,{\rm rel\,}}
. We define the fine version of the relative spectrum, denoted by
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{R,{\rm fine\,}}(A)
, analogously.

Remark 5.23
.

Once again the fine Zariski topology is strictly finer than the Zariski topology (but not functorial in general), and we obtain a canonical continuous map

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{R,{\rm fine\,}}(A)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{R}(A).

It is clear from the above discussion that the topological space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{R}(A)
does not only depend on the choice of
A
but crucially also on the choice of the base commutative ring
R
.

Proposition 5.24
.

Given a finitely presented commutative
R
-algebra
A
, we have a canonical homeomorphism

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{R,{\rm fine\,}}(A)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)

of topological spaces.

Proof.

To establish that the relative noncommutative fine spectrum agrees with the Grothendieck spectrum, we observe that

R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\cong\frac{R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle}{(x_{1}x_{2}-x_{2}x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}x_{n}-x_{n}x_{n-1})}

is clearly a finitely presented
R
-algebra in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Thus, all localizations of the classical Zariski spectrum are localizations in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{R}
. Finally, arguing as in Proposition
5.21
, one concludes that these are the only possible localizations. By Theorem
5.19
(and argiung as in Corollary
5.20
) the notions of cover for the two topologies coincide.
∎

The relative spectrum could be useful to understand the “local” geometry more easily compared to the “global” perspective adopted in this work. The next example clarifies this heuristic remark.

Example 5.25
.

Let
R={\mathbb{C}}
, which is not finitely generated over
{\mathbb{Z}}
. For any finitely generated
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra
A
we have

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\mathbb{Z}}(A)=\star

but

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{{\mathbb{C}},{\rm fine\,}}(A)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A).

5.4  A few properties of the noncommutative spectrum

We conclude this section by discussing a few basic properties of the new notion of spectrum defined above.

Proposition 5.26
.

The following properties hold.

1.

If
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)\not\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A_{1})\coprod{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A_{2})
, but
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A_{1})\coprod{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A_{2})
as topological spaces.

2.

If
A\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a domain
22
22

22

It is customary to call rings without zero divisors
domains
and commutative rings without zero divisors
integral domains
.
, then
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
is connected.

3.

More generally, if we have two morphisms
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
and
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(C)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
, then the fiber product exists and is computed as
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}C)
, which usually does not agree with
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)\times_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)}{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(C)
as topological space.

4.

If for
A,B\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
are derived Morita equivalent (
i.e.

{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\cong{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}
), then
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(B)
.

Proof.

1.

The split cofiber sequence

{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A_{1}}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A_{2}},

identifies
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A_{1}}
with the kernel of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A_{2}}
, and a similar argument gives the opposite inclusion. This shows that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\cong{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A_{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A_{2}}
.
Hence, the projections
A\to A_{1}
and
A\to A_{2}
are both homotopical epimorphisms and kernels of homotopical epimorphisms. Their intersection is clearly empty. So, they determine subsets of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
that are both closed and open, and form a cover for the fine Zariski topology. Example
5.1
demonstrates that the two projections might not be covers for the homotopy Zariski topology.

2.

Similarly to the previous item, a closed and open subset of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
is uniquely determined by a smashing subcategory that is also a smashing localization. Moreover, this determines a decomposition of
A
as a direct product of two subrings. Such a decomposition cannot exist if
A
is a domain.

3.

Since
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is by definition the opposite category of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, fiber products in
{\mathbf{dAff}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
correspond to pushouts in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The latter are given by the (derived) free product. It is not hard to come up with examples for which
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}B)\not\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)\times{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)
as the isomorphism rarely holds, already in classical algebraic geometry. Examples of a similar type can be devised for noncommutative spectra as well.

4.

By Proposition
5.16
the lattice of localizations of
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
depends only on
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
. The notion of cover for the fine Zariski topology also depends on the lattice of localizations only, because for two homotopical epimoprhisms
A\to B
,
A\to C
, one has
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}C}={\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}\cap{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{C}
where the intersection happen inside
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
.

∎

Further analysis is required to understand more properties of the functor
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}
and the spectrum
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}
. For instance, it is natural to wonder whether given
A,B\in{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
are that are derived Morita equivalent, then one has
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)
. We do not know whether this is true in general, because even if the localizations of
A
and
B
depend only of the derived category, the notion of cover, in principle, does not.

The analysis performed in this paper lays the foundations of the study of noncommutative geometry over
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. So far, only affine objects have been considered; this is fine until one wishes to construct new spaces by gluing spaces together. In classical algebraic geometry this is done by computing colimits in the category of sheaves for the Zariski topology. And the natural approach would be to do the same in the noncommutative setting as well. This is not possible because representable pre-sheaves associated to noncommutative rings are not sheaves for the homotopical Zariski topology. But this has not to be considered as a problem of our theory, it is actually an interesting feature whose study we start in Section
7
. We will see that noncommutative spaces manifest a local-to-global behaviour that is not fully captured by the notion of sheaf although they are objects that can be descended from covers of the underlying topological space.

6  Examples of noncommutative spectra

We now discuss some explicit examples of noncommutative spectra. We will examine relatively simple cases, as an illustration of the theory developed in this paper. A more thorough examination of the consequences of our new notion of noncommutative spectrum goes beyond the scope of the present paper and will be carried out in upcoming publications.
Let us emphasise that the computation of the spectrum requires the classification of smashing subcategories of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
, which is usually not an easy task and an open problem for most classes of rings.
We plan to discuss further examples and provide additional details about the computations in future publications.

6.1  Commutative rings

Before moving on to noncommutative rings, we give a few key examples of noncommutative spectra of commutative rings. These can be compared with the classical notion of spectrum introduced by Grothendieck, as per Proposition
5.10
; we will now see that they differ, except for very specific cases.

Commutative fields.

Let
k
be a commutative field
k
. The class of smashing localizations of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{k}
only contains trivial objects; hence,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)
and its underlying space is just a point.

Discrete valuation rings.

This is the case, for example, of the ring of formal power series
R=k[\![x]\!]
in one variable
x
over some field
k
, or of the ring of p-adic integers
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
for any prime
p
.
The class of homotopy epimorphisms under
R
, by the classification results from
[
2
, Theorem 1.1 (2)]
, contains only three elements: the identity
R\to R
, the localization
R\to{\rm Frac}(R)
, and
R\to 0
. All localizations are of finite presentation (in the commutative sense), so also in this case
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
, and its underlying topological space is the usual Sierpinski space. Also in this case we have
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(R)
.

The ring
k^{n}
.

Let
k
be a field. Consider the direct product
k^{n}=k\times\cdots\times k
. Then, by generalising Example
5.1
, one computes
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)
to be the boolean algebra of subsets of the set with
n
elements equipped with the trivial topology. Therefore,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)
has
2^{n}-n-1
specialization points and
n
generic points.
On the other hand,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k)
is the set of
n
points with the discrete topology.

Noetherian commutative rings.

This is the case, for example, of the ring of formal power series
R=N[\![x]\!]
over a Noetherian ring
N
. The smashing localizations of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{R}
have been classified in
[
2
, Theorem 1.1 (2)]
. These correspond precisely to flat ring epimorphisms
R\to S
. Thus, for such an
R
the category
{\mathbf{Loc}}_{R}
is just the class of all flat ring epimorphisms whose domain is
R
. Note that in this case
S
is necessarily commutative (see
[
56
, Corollary 1.2]
). Moreover, flat ring epimorphisms
R\to S
determine generalization
closed subsets of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
, as these are arbitrary intersections of open subsets (see
[
57
, Lemma 5.19.3]
). Therefore,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
is obtained from
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
by enlarging the family of compact open subset to encompass the family of all generalization closed subsets that are images of flat ring epimorphisms. Then, the cover condition for
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
is difficult to check and not compatible with the geometry of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
in general, as Example
5.1
shows. Instead, Theorem
5.19
shows that the cover condition for the topology of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(R)
is that the family of generalization closed subsets covers
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)
set-theoretically.

Let us focus on the special case
R={\mathbb{Z}}
. The classification of smashing localization of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
performed in
[
2
, Theorem 1.1 (2)]
implies that, in the case at hand, smashing localizations correspond to morphisms
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}]
, where
S^{-1}
is a subset of primes of
{\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore, in this case the category
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}})}
is a distributive lattice, where

{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}])\wedge{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[T^{-1}])\cong{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Z}}[T^{-1}])\cong{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[(S\cup T)^{-1}])

and

{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}])\lor{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[T^{-1}])\cong{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}}[(S\cap T)^{-1}]).

Now, upon establishing a correspondence between
{\mathbb{N}}
and the set of prime numbers, we can view objects of
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}})}
as subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}
, equipped with the order relation given by the opposite of inclusion and the lattice operations given by finite intersections and arbitrary unions. Therefore, the frame
{\rm Id\,}({\mathbf{Zar}}_{{\rm Spec}({\mathbb{Z}})})
is the frame of all ideals of the lattice of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}
plus the extra element corresponding to the trivial localization
{\mathbb{Z}}\to 0
.
The topology of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})
can be described as follows: a subset
C\subset{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})
is closed if there exists
S\in{\mathscr{P}}({\mathbb{N}})
such that

C=\{x\in{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})\ |\ S\in x\}.

In this particular case we have agreement between the commutative and noncommutative notions of cover because one can prove the following canonical isomorphism:
{\mathbb{Z}}\langle X\rangle\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}]\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\langle X\rangle\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}]
for all sets of variables
X
and all subset
S
of the prime numbers. This implies that for
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
one has
A\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}]\cong 0
if and only if
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{-1}]\cong 0
, and hence
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}({\mathbb{Z}})
because the cover conditions in the two situation happen to agree.
This means that, as a set,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})
corresponds to the set of ultrafilters of
{\mathbb{N}}
, plus an extra generic point.
This is a Zariski-like topology and it is not Hausdorff. An easy way to see that it is not Hausdorff is by observing that
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)})
is not Hausdorff, and the latter embeds into
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})
via the open localization
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}
.

We think that the given homeomorphism
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}({\mathbb{Z}})
is very specific to
{\mathbb{Z}}
; in fact, we conjecture that this already fails for the affine line
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
. We postpone a deeper study of these example until future work.

Non-Noetherian commutative rings.

Let
R
be the non-Noetherian valuation domain considered in
[
15
, Example 5.24]
. If
\mathfrak{m}\subset R
denotes the maximal ideal of
R
then
\mathfrak{m}^{2}=\mathfrak{m}
and the morphism
R\to\frac{R}{\mathfrak{m}}
is a non-flat homotopy epimorphism. In
[
15
, Example 5.24]
it is explained that
R\to\frac{R}{\mathfrak{m}}
,
R\to\rm{Frac}(R)
and
R\to\frac{R}{\mathfrak{m}}\times\rm{Frac}(R)
are the only non-trivial homotopical epimorphisms of
R
. If we denote
k=\frac{R}{\mathfrak{m}}
and
Q=\rm{Frac}(R)
we get the distributive lattice of homotopical epimorphisms

\hbox to151.74pt{\vbox to141.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 75.86949pt\lower-70.7345pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

The associated sober topological space for the fine Zariski topology has two generic points
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
that share a common closed special points
s
, as per the following picture:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

In this representation, the open subsets of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(R)=\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},s\}
are
{\rm\varnothing}
,
\{\eta_{1}\}
,
\{\eta_{2}\}
,
\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
,
\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},s\}
. Thus,
s
is a closed but not open point. Let us observe that the closure of
\{\eta_{1}\}
is the irreducible closed subset
\{\eta_{1},s\}
and the closure of
\{\eta_{2}\}
is the irreducible closed subset
\{\eta_{2},s\}
. Therefore
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(R)
is not an irreducible topological space even if
R
is an integral domain, unlike what happens in scheme theory. Finally, the projection

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(R)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(R)

can be interpreted as the projection onto one of the closed irreducible subspaces
\{\eta_{1},s\}
sending
\eta_{2}
to
\eta_{1}
. It is straightforward to check that this map is continuous.

6.2  Noncommutative rings

The noncommutative examples are more challenging to work out, and only few are currently known to us.

The ring
M_{n}(k)
of square matrices over a field
k
.

The first example is a trivial one. Since
M_{n}(k)
is derived Morita equivalent to
k
, and by Proposition
5.26
the noncommutative spectrum depends only on the derived category of left (or right) modules, we have that
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(M_{n}(k))\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(M_{n}(k))\cong{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)
is a one point topological space.

Division rings.

Let
k
be a division algebra. It is well known that the category
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{k}
is formally identical to the category of vector spaces over a field: all objects are free, and hence projective. This immediately implies that
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{k}
has only trivial smashing localizations, like in the case of fields. So,
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)
is a singleton and, by the Morita invariance of the spectrum, so is
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(M_{n}(k))
.

Path algebra for the quiver
A_{n}
.

Let us consider the path algebra for the quiver
A_{n}
(over a field
k
) and denote it by
kA_{n}
. It is known that
kA_{n}
has only a finite number of smashing localizations. In this example, we focus on the special case
kA_{2}
; the analysis for
kA_{n}
,
n>2
, is similar.

We refer the reader to
[
33
, Example 5.1.4]
for the classification of the smashing subcategories of
kA_{2}
, from which we borrow the notation. Smashing subcategories of
kA_{2}
form the lattice

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces kA_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

which is well-known to be non-distributive.
One can show that that only trivial covers are covers for the homotopy Zariski topology. We refer to Example
6.2
for a proof of this fact. Now, the only covers for the homotopy Zariski topology in this case are
\{{\rm Id\,}_{0}\}
,
\{{\rm Id\,}_{P_{1}}\}
,
\{{\rm Id\,}_{P_{2}}\}
,
\{{\rm Id\,}_{S_{2}}\}
, and
\{{\rm Id\,}_{kA_{2}}\}
. Hence the list of all ideals for this topology is

0:=\{0\},\ 01:=\{0,P_{1}\},\ 02:=\{0,P_{2}\},\ 03:=\{0,S_{2}\},

\ 012:=\{0,P_{1},P_{2}\},\ 013:=\{0,P_{1},S_{2}\},\ 023:=\{0,P_{2},S_{2}\}

\ 0123=\{0,P_{1},P_{2},S_{2}\},\ 01234:=\{0,P_{1},P_{2},S_{2},kA_{2}\}.
