It is then easy to check that
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2})
is the spectral space

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

with three generic points that share a common specialization point.

In order to describe
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(kA_{2})
, we observe that the family
\{kA_{2}\to A_{P_{1}},kA_{2}\to A_{P_{2}},kA_{2}\to A_{S_{2}}\}
is a non-trivial faithful family of functors. The presence of this extra cover has the effect of removing
0123
from the list of ideals of the posite.
It is not hard to check that the topological space associated with this latter frame is the discrete topological space with three points. More generally, we expect
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(kA_{n})
to be a discrete space whose points correspond to indecomposable finite dimensional representations of
A_{n}
, and
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{n})
to be the spectral topological space associated to the (finite) lattice of smashing localizations of
kA_{n}
equipped with the trivial posite structure. Similar considerations can be made for quiver algebras of finite representation type.

Kronecker algebra.

Let us consider the Kronecker algebra
A
over a field
k
. This algebra can be defined as the path algebra of the (Kronecker) quiver
\star\rightrightarrows\star
, and is isomorphic to the matrix algebra

\begin{pmatrix}k&k^{2}\\
0&k\end{pmatrix}\,,

that is, a four dimensional noncommutative algebra over the field
k
. Owing to the work of Beilinson
[
18
]
, we know that there is an explicit equivalence of categories

D({\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{Coh}}({\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{1}))\cong D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})\,,

where on the left-hand side we have the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{1}
.
The smashing subcategories of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
have been classified in
[
38
]
. Since
A
is hereditary, the smashing subcategories of
D({\mathbf{Mod}}_{A})
correspond to those of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
(because it is known that in this case homotopical epimorphisms are always in degree
0
,
i.e.
given by ring epimorphisms, see
[
15
]
). So, by
[
38
]
we obtain the poset

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

which is not distributive, and where on the left part of the diagram we have depicted the lattice of localizations corresponding to specialisation closed subsets of
{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{1}
. The non-distributivity is due to the presence of sub-lattices of the form

The topological space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
can be described analogously to

above, so we omit the details. The final outcome of the construction is that there exists infinitely many points corresponding to the twisting sheaves

and another component whose points correspond to ultrafilters of rational points of
{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{1}
plus a generic point (similarly to what has been described for
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})
).

6.3  Morphisms of spectra

Before moving on to discuss our noncommutative version of Gelfand’s duality, let us give a couple of relevant examples of continuous maps between noncommutative spectra.

The first example, stems from Example
5.1
.

Example 6.1
.

As in Example
5.1
, consider a field
k
and the diagonal inclusion morphism
\phi:k\oplus k\to M_{2}(k)
. The latter morphism induces a morphism of spectra
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(M_{2}(k))\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
that we now describe. We have already seen that

M_{2}(k)\ast_{k\oplus k}^{\mathbb{L}}k\cong 0
for both projections
k\oplus k\to k
. Moreover, the isomorphism
M_{2}(k)\ast_{k\oplus k}^{\mathbb{L}}k\oplus k\cong M_{2}(k)
is obvious. Therefore, the morphism
\phi
induces a functor

{\mathbf{Ouv}}(k\oplus k)\to{\mathbf{Ouv}}(M_{2}(k))

that is non-trivial outside the generic points of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
. More precisely, one can check that this means that the prime filter corresponding to the point of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(M_{2}(k))
is sent to the prime filter corresponding to the specialization point of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
.

Let us complete the picture by considering also the inclusion morphism
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k\oplus k)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
. If we do so, we obtain the following diagram of continuous maps (solid arrows)

\hbox to213.99pt{\vbox to61.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 106.99388pt\lower-30.97061pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-103.66087pt}{-23.15417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -143.44 -32.04)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-54.1248pt}{-1.75pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -74.89 -2.42)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{}{}{}}{}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.6563pt}{23.99622pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 3.68 33.2)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here the dashed horizontal map cannot be realised as a map of topological spaces because the image of the map
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(M_{2}(k))\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
lies outside the image of the map
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k\oplus k)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
. Therefore, the dashed arrow can only be the empty relation.

Let us observe that even if there is no continuous map
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(M_{2}(k))\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k\oplus k)
, we can always consider a sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k\oplus k)
, push it to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
, and pull it back to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(M_{2}(k))
— and the other way around: push forward sheaves from
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(M_{2}(k))
to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k\oplus k)
and then pullback to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(k\oplus k)
, thus obtaining a rich enough geometrical setting.

In the next example we show that the homotopical Zariski topology induces the trivial topology on the poset of smashing localizations of
kA_{2}
.

Example 6.2
.

Consider the inclusion morphism

kA_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}k&0\\
k&k\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}k&0\\
(k,0)&k\end{pmatrix}\to A.

By functoriality, this induces a morphism of spectra

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2}).

Now, suppose that the topology on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(kA_{2})
is non-trivial. This means, borrowing the notation from Example
6.1
, that the family
\{kA_{2}\to A_{P_{1}},kA_{2}\to A_{P_{2}},kA_{2}\to A_{S_{2}}\}
must be a cover, as it is the only non-trivial faithful family of localization functors. But then the morphism
kA_{2}\to A
must map this cover of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(kA_{2})
to a cover of
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
. However,
A
does not have any finite family of faithful functors. Therefore,
\{kA_{2}\to A_{P_{1}},kA_{2}\to A_{P_{2}},kA_{2}\to A_{S_{2}}\}
is not a cover for the homotopical Zariski topology on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2})
.

Elaborating further on this example (by considering homotopical epimorphisms of
A
that are disjoint from the image of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2})
into
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
), one can provide explicit instances of non-zero algebras whose derived free product over
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2})
with all
A_{P_{1}},A_{P_{2}},A_{S_{2}}
is zero, giving a more direct proof of the fact that
\{kA_{2}\to A_{P_{1}},kA_{2}\to A_{P_{2}},kA_{2}\to A_{S_{2}}\}
does not satisfy the cover condition for the homotopical Zariski topology. We leave these further details to the reader.

7  Towards a noncommutative notion of sheaf

Gelfand’s duality realises a duality between the category of compact Hausdorff topological spaces and the category of commutative
C^{*}
-algebras. Some years after Gelfand’s work, Grothendieck observed that, in order to obtain a similar duality for commutative rings, some extra data is needed. Indeed, for example, if we just regard the spectrum of a commutative ring as a topological space, we cannot tell any field apart from any other: all their spectra are isomorphic to the singleton topological space. The extra data required to realise a precise duality between commutative rings and their spectra is the structural sheaf of rings whose stalks at any point are local rings. The datum of a topological space equipped with such a structure sheaf is called a
locally ringed space
. Grothendieck has shown that the category of affine schemes embeds fully faithfully into the category of locally ringed spaces, realising an algebraic version of Gelfand’s duality.

Our goal is to achieve a similar result in the noncommutative setting using the notion of spectrum introduced above. Since we are in the homotopical setting, we would like to replace the classical notion of sheaf with its homotopical version commonly used in derived algebraic geometry. However, the noncommutative situation is more complicated for a number of reasons. For starters, we have considered two spectra so far:
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
and
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
.
Finally — and most crucially — one cannot directly use the theory of (homotopical) comonadic descent as in (derived) algebraic geometry.

We shall now perform an examination of these issues in some detail.

7.1  Descent and sheaf theory

In this subsection we would like start investigating applications of sheaf theory to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
and
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
. The main issue we will face is that we can provide an example of noncommutative ring where the natural structure pre-sheaf is a sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
but its pullback (as a pre-sheaf) to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
is not a sheaf. Furthermore, its sheafification does not satisfy the properties a structure sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
should possess. This motives us to generalise the notion of sheaf using the theory of comonadic descent.

We start by discussing an example which shows that the natural definition of the structure pre-sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
is not compatible with the fine Zariski topology, in the sense that it is not a sheaf for this topology.

Example 7.1

(Sheafyness problem)
.

Let us consider again
A=kA_{2}
, the path algebra of the
A_{2}
quiver. In subsection
6.2
we have seen that
X={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
is the discrete space with three points. These three points, being open subsets, correspond to three homotopical epimorphisms
A\to k
,
A\to M_{2}(k)
and
A\to k
(see Example
7.2
below for more information about how to compute these homotopical epimorphisms). Thus, the structural pre-sheaf is given by

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(p_{1})=k,\ \ {\mathcal{O}}_{X}(p_{2})=M_{2}(k),\ \ {\mathcal{O}}_{X}(p_{3})=k.

If this pre-sheaf were a sheaf, then one would have that the cover of
X
made by singletons gives

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(X)=k\oplus M_{2}(k)\oplus k\,;

but
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(X)=A
, and the latter has dimension
3
as
k
-vector space.

This shows that the natural definition of the structural pre-sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
does not satisfy in general the sheaf condition on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
when
A
is not commutative. However, we know that the covers of
X
for the homotopical Zariski topology induce faithful, hence conservative, functors at the level of base changes of modules (see Proposition
5.3
). We will show that this implies that such family of functors satisfy descent (in the homotopical sense). Therefore, there is still a local-to-global principle that the pre-sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
over
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
and modules over it satisfy. A
localization and reconstruction of the data of such pre-sheaves does not follow the rules of sheaf theory but those of comonadic descent (of which sheaf theory is a particular case). Let us elaborate on this point for the special case of the path algebra of the
A_{2}
quiver, before addressing the abstract theory.

Example 7.2

(Descent for the quiver
A_{2}
)
.

Let us examine the example of
A=kA_{2}
and its localizations more closely.
The algebra
A
over a field
k
is isomorphic to the algebra of lower triangular matrices of the form

A=\begin{pmatrix}k&0\\
k&k\end{pmatrix}.

Let us denote a
k
-basis of
A
by
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
, so that

A=\begin{pmatrix}ke_{1}&0\\
ke_{2}&ke_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

We denote

P_{1}=ke_{3},\ \ P_{2}=ke_{1}\oplus ke_{2},\ \ S_{2}=ke_{1}.

Note that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are projective left
A
-modules,
A\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{2}
as a left
A
-module, whereas
S_{2}
has the left
A
-module structure induced by the quotient map
P_{2}\to S_{2}
and it is obviously indecomposable. These submodules correspond to indecomposable representations of
A_{2}
and to generators of the (non-trivial) smashing subcategories of
{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}
, as discussed in subsection
6.2
.
We also have the right
A
-modules

Q_{1}=ke_{1},\ \ Q_{2}=ke_{2}\oplus ke_{3},\ \ T_{2}=ke_{3}\,,

whose description is specular to that of
P_{1},P_{2},S_{2}
. We also have two surjective epimorphisms of algebras

A\to P_{1}=ke_{3}

and

A\to S_{2}=ke_{1}.

It is straightforward to check that these are homotopical epimorphisms that correspond to the localizations at
P_{1}
and
S_{2}
considered in subsection
6.2
.
The third and last homotopical epimorphism of
A
is given by the inclusion morphism

A\to M_{2}(k).

The
A
-bimodule structure on
M_{2}(k)
is given by regarding the latter as two copies of
P_{2}
as a left module and two copies of
Q_{2}
as right module. These actions are compatible with matrix multiplication of
M_{2}(k)
. Again, checking that this morphism is a homotopical epimorphism can be done by means of straightforward (albeit tedious) computations or one can appeal to more powerful abstract theories. We thus get the canonical morphism of
A
-algebras

A\to k\oplus M_{2}(k)\oplus k

mentioned in Example
7.1
. If we denote the three localizations of
{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}
corresponding to localizing at
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
S_{2}
by
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
respectively, we get

i_{1}(P_{1})=P_{1},\ \ i_{1}(P_{2})=0,\ \ i_{1}(S_{2})=P_{1}\,,

and thus
{\rm Ker\,}(i_{1})=\langle P_{2}\rangle
. Here and further on, we denote by
\langle\scalebox{0.55}[1.0]{$-$}\rangle
the smashing localization generated by the elements within the brackets. Analogously, we have

i_{2}(P_{1})=P_{2},\ \ i_{2}(P_{2})=P_{2},\ \ i_{2}(S_{2})=0,

so that
{\rm Ker\,}(i_{2})=\langle S_{2}\rangle
and

i_{3}(P_{1})=0,\ \ i_{3}(P_{2})=S_{2},\ \ i_{3}(S_{2})=S_{2},

so that

{\rm Ker\,}(i_{3})=\langle P_{1}\rangle
. Concretely,
i_{1}
is obtained by tensoring by the bimodule
ke_{3}
,
i_{2}
by the bimodule
M_{2}(k)
, and
i_{3}
by the bimodule
ke_{1}
. If we now go back to the discrete topological space
X={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
, we would expect the intersection of any two points to be empty. Indeed, if we regard
A\to P_{1}
as corresponding to the point
p_{1}
, then one can check that

P_{1}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}P_{2}\cong 0,\ \ P_{1}\ast_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}S_{2}\cong 0.

But the above formula show that

P_{1}\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}P_{2}\cong 0,\ \ P_{1}\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}S_{2}\cong P_{1}.

So,
p_{1}
and
p_{3}
are not “completely disjoint” inside
{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}
. This does not, however, prevent us from reconstructing
A
(and hence every
A
-module) from the data of the three homotopical epimorphisms above.

Let us define the functor

M:{}_{A}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}\to{}_{k}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}\oplus{}_{M_{2}(k)}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}\oplus{}_{k}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}

given by
M(R)=(i_{1}(R),i_{2}(R),i_{3}(R))
, that is, the base change functor of the algebra
A\to k\oplus M_{2}(k)\oplus k
. Then, we have three adjunction maps: the map

\eta_{P_{1}}:P_{1}\to M(P_{1})=i_{1}(P_{1})\oplus i_{2}(P_{1})\oplus i_{3}(P_{1})=P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\,,

a\mapsto(a,\alpha(a))\,,

where the inclusion
\alpha:P_{1}\to P_{2}
is defined as
e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}
. Thus

{\rm Coker\,}(\alpha)\cong S_{2}.

The map

\eta_{P_{2}}:P_{2}\to M(P_{2})=i_{1}(P_{2})\oplus i_{2}(P_{2})\oplus i_{3}(P_{2})=P_{2}\oplus S_{2}\,,

a\mapsto(a,\beta(a))\,,

where
\beta:P_{2}\to S_{2}
is the quotient map whose kernel is
\alpha
. And finally the map

\eta_{S_{2}}:S_{2}\to M(S_{2})=i_{1}(S_{2})\oplus i_{2}(S_{2})\oplus i_{3}(S_{2})=P_{1}\oplus S_{2}\,,

a\mapsto(0,a)\,.

Applying the functor again, we obtain

M(M(A))=M(M(P_{1}\oplus P_{2}))=M(P_{1})\oplus M(P_{2}^{2})\oplus M(S_{2})=P_{1}^{2}\oplus P_{2}^{3}\oplus S_{2}^{3}.

Now, we have two maps

M(A)\rightrightarrows M(M(A))

given by
\eta_{M(A)}
and
M(\eta_{A})
.
The map

\eta_{A}:A=P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to M(A)=P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus S_{2}

is given by

(a,b)=(a,\alpha(a),b,\beta(b)).

We have that the identity
\eta_{A}=\eta_{P_{1}}\oplus\eta_{P_{2}}
gives

M(\eta_{A})=M(\eta_{P_{1}})\oplus M(\eta_{P_{2}})

and

\eta_{P_{1}}=({\rm id\,}_{P_{1}}\oplus\alpha)\circ\Delta_{P_{1}}\,

where
\Delta_{P_{1}}:P_{1}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{1}
is the diagonal embedding. So,

M(\eta_{P_{1}})=M(({\rm id\,}_{P_{1}}\oplus\alpha)\circ\Delta_{P_{1}})=({\rm id\,}_{M(P_{1})},M(\alpha))\circ\Delta_{M(P_{1})}.

and

M(\alpha)=({\rm id\,}_{P_{2}},0).

So,

M(\eta_{P_{1}})(a,b)=(a,b,b,0).

Similarly, one can compute that

M(\eta_{P_{2}}):P_{2}\oplus S_{2}\to P_{2}\oplus S_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus S_{2}

M(\eta_{P_{2}})(c,d)=(c,d,0,d).

On the other hand,

\eta_{M(A)}:P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus S_{2}\to P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus S_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus S_{2}\oplus P_{1}\oplus S_{2}

\eta_{M(A)}(a,b,c,d)=(a,\alpha(a),b,\beta(b),c,\beta(c),0,d).

Therefore,

\eta_{M(A)}-M(\eta_{A})=(0,\alpha(a)-b,0,\beta(b),0,d-\beta(c),0,0).

All in all, we have the sequence

A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{A}}}{{\to}}M(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{M(A)}-M(\eta_{A})}}{{\longrightarrow}}{M(M(A))}\,.

Let us recall that

\eta_{A}(a,b)=(a,\alpha(a),b,\beta(b))

is injective. So, it is clear that
\eta_{A}(A)\subset{\rm Ker\,}(\eta_{M(A)}-M(\eta_{A}))
. The other inclusion is also easy to check. This shows that
A\cong{\rm Ker\,}(\eta_{M(A)}-M(\eta_{A}))
, so that the above sequence of morphisms is exact at
M(A)
.

The computations performed in Example
7.2
are not accidental: they can be explained through the theory of comonadic descent, which gives the correct formulas for reconstructing
A
-modules from their local components at a cover for the fine Zariski topology that is faithful.
In the remainder of this section, we shall partially develop the abstract theory. We shall limit ourselves to the algebraic aspects of the theory here, deferring the development of a fully fledged geometric theory until future work. Thus, to use the machinery from comonadic descent one needs to know that covers for the fine Zariski topology are faithful,
i.e.
Conjecture
5.18
. From now on we assume this conjecture to be true, since it is verified in all examples we know.

Assumption 7.3
.

In what follows we assume that Conjecture
5.18
is true.

We would like to use covers for the fine Zariski topology to write the category of modules as a finite limit, as in
[
53
, Proposition 10.5]
.
Let us immediately warn the reader that the latter proposition is stated in the language of
\infty
-categories, which would be the best language suited to the formulation of the results of this section. However, we will stick with our choice, made (and justified) earlier in the paper, of avoiding the language of
\infty
-categories in this work, even if at times this somewhat hinders the clarity of the results.

In our language, for the topological space
X={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
with
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and given homotopical epimorphism
A\to B_{U}
,
[
53
, Proposition 10.5]
provides sufficient conditions under which the association

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(B_{U})=U\mapsto{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{U}}\,,

is a homotopical sheaf of pre-triangulated categories.
This condition implies that the association

U\mapsto B_{U}

is a homotopical sheaf of rings, which means, in turn, that given a cover
\{A\to B_{U_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
for the homotopical Zariski topology we have

A\cong{\mathbb{R}}\lim N^{\bullet}(B_{U_{i}})\,.

Here
N^{\bullet}(B_{U_{i}})
denotes the C̆ech nerve of the cover, often referred to
as
cobar construction
, given by the cosimplicial object

\prod_{i\in I}B_{U_{i}}\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}B_{U_{i}}\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{U_{j}}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\end{subarray}}\cdots.

Note that condition (i) in
[
53
, Proposition 10.5]
essentially never holds unless
A
is either a commutative ring or a cdga, because it would require the tensor product (of the underlying bimodules) of algebras to be commutative. In particular, it does not hold for the cover for the fine Zariski topology of
kA_{2}
as we have seen in Example
7.2
, hence the failure to obtain a homotopical sheaf. But
[
53
, Proposition 10.5]
has another interpretation in terms of comonadic descent. Indeed, its statement implies that, under the given hypothesis, the adjunction

(7.1)

{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\leftrightarrows\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{U_{i}}}

defined by base change and restriction of scalars functors, is homotopically comonadic. The additional piece of information given by (the proof of)
[
53
, Proposition 10.5]
is that under the proposition’s hypothesis, the descent data is given by a finite diagram
23
23

23

The theory of
\infty
-categories makes this statement very clear. We do not comment further here.
. In our situation instead we still have homotopical descent, but we shall see that the descent data is no longer contained in a finite diagram, and the resulting cobar construction of modules and algebras over
A
are not equal to the restriction of section of sheaves on the topological space
X
as in the commutative case. It is always possible to reconstruct a module
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
uniquely from its localizations
M\otimes_{A}B_{U_{i}}
if the cover is faithful, and, furthermore, any descent data on such a cover
\{A\to B_{U_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
, formally given by a comodule over the comonad associated to the adjunction (
7.1
), comes from a module over
A
. Thus, our goal is to prove that the adjunction (
7.1
) is comonadic. To do this we introduce the following generalisation (and adaptation) of the terminology introduced in
[
44
]
in the commutative setting.

Definition 7.4
.

Consider
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and an
A
-algebra
A\to B
. We say that
B

admits descent
or is
descendable
if the smallest smashing subcategory of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
containing
B
is the whole
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
.

Remark 7.5
.

Let us point out that it is easy to check that if
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover for the homotopy Zariski topology, then the
A
-algebra
A\to\prod_{i\in I}B_{U_{i}}
is descendable. Indeed, by Proposition
5.3
we have that the family of functors
\{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}\}
is faithful and in particular conservative, which implies that the smallest smashing subcategory containing
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}
in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
— and hence the one generated by
\prod_{i\in I}B_{i}
— is the whole
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
. For the fine Zariski topology the faithfulness of the covers are ensured by Assumption
7.3
.

Let us now show how to recontruct
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
from the data of a descendable algebra
A\to B
. We will follow closely the discussion from
[
44
, Section 3]
.

Proposition 7.6
.

An
A
-algebra
A\to B
is descendable if and only if the canonical map

A\to{\mathbb{R}}\lim N^{\bullet}(B)

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

This is a generalisation of
[
44
, Proposition 3.20]
. One can check that the proof never uses the commutativity of the algebras but only the canonical morphisms of the extension of scalars and restriction of scalars functors. Therefore, it can be applied also for the adjunction
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}
verbatim.
∎

The next theorem shows how comonadic descent is related to homotopy limits.

Theorem 7.7
.

If the
A
-algebra
A\to B
is descendable, then the adjunction

{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B}

is comonadic.

Proof.

It is enough to check that it is possible to apply the Lurie–Barr–Beck Theorem. This is done as in
[
44
, Proposition 3.22]
, where only abstract results from the theory of comonadic descent are used.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

Let us emphasise that we have been deliberately sketchy in the proofs of Theorem
7.7
and Proposition
7.6
, because there exists another, more classical, way to obtain (essentially) the same results (under the stronger hypotheses that we will impose later on). Indeed, by the work of Balmer
[
5
]
, we know that all faithful monads between triangulated categories satisfy descent
24
24

24

Here we employ Balmer’s terminology on descent. Given a comonad
M:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
, in Balmer’s terminology,
M
is said to satisfy
descent
if the comparison functor
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{Co}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{M}
is fully faithful and it is said to satisfy
effective descent
if the comparison functor is an equivalence. We refer to
[
5
]
for details on these constructions. What we are calling comonadic descent in the present paper corresponds to Balmer’s effective descent.
— see
[
5
, Corollary 2.15]
. Observe that Balmer’s result can be stated by replacing the
{\mathbb{R}}\lim N^{\bullet}(B)
with the
\lim N^{\bullet}(B)
, which has the effect of “switching” from the homotopical descent to the usual categorical descent. Therefore, Balmer’s computations in
[
5
, Corollary 2.15]
effectively represent the first step of the proof Theorem
7.7
, under the faithfulness hypothesis. By iterating Balmer’s computations, one obtains the full homotopical comonadic descent. Let us also emphasise that Balmer’s computations yield the even stronger result that the complex obtained via comonadic descent in the faithful situation is not only exact but split exact.

In our situation, the meaning of Theorem
7.7
is that the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
can be reconstructed as a homotopical limit of the categories
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}
. Thus, by following the prescription of comonadic descent, it is possible to reconstruct the global data (
i.e.

{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
) from the local data (
i.e.
the localizations
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{i}}
). But this reconstruction process does not give a sheaf in the usual sense on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
, because in the noncommutative case the base change of algebras is not compatible with the base change of modules (concretely, Theorem
5.19
fails).

However, it is not difficult to check that it is still a well-defined pre-sheaf, as it is obvious by the property of stability of homotopical epimorphisms under composition. A less obvious statement is that for any object
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
the associatied pre-sheaf is well-defined. For the sake of completeness, we provide a proof of this basic fact below.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
M\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
. Then the association

\widetilde{M}(U)=M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{U}

is a pre-sheaf valued in
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
, on the site
{\mathbf{Ouv}}({\rm Spec}(A))
, where
U
is the open subset corresponding to the homotopical epimorphism
A\to B_{U}
.

Proof.

Being a pre-sheaf just means being a functor
{\mathbf{Loc}}(A)\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. So, the task at hand reduces to check that composition is well-defined. Let
A\to B_{U}
,
B_{U}\to C_{V}
be two homotopical epimorphism. Then we have the natural isomorphism

\widetilde{M}(V)=M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}C_{V}\cong M\otimes_{A}^{\mathbb{L}}B_{U}\otimes_{B_{U}}^{\mathbb{L}}C_{V}\,.

This yields the claim.
∎

From Proposition
7.9
it is not hard to deduce the following more refined result.

Corollary 7.10
.

The association

U\mapsto{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{B_{U}}

defines a pre-sheaf
25
25

25

Technically this is a 2-to-1 pseudofunctor, because it is valued in the category of additive categories. We do not dwell into the details of the theory of 2-to-1 pseudofunctors here, because their use is not needed in the framework of
\infty
-categories.
of categories on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
.

With this in mind, we are in the position to provide a new notion of
descendable pre-sheaf of rings.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
X
be a topological space (or more generally a site).
A
descendable pre-sheaf of rings

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}:{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)^{\rm op\,}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a contravariant functor from the category of open sets of
X
to homotopical rings
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying the
descent condition
that for any cover
\{U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
such that
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
we have

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(U)={\mathbb{R}}\lim N^{\bullet}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U_{i}})\right)\,,

where
N^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U_{i}}))
denotes the Amistur-C̆ech nerve of the cover given by the cosimplicial object

\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U_{i}})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U_{i}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U})}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U_{j}})\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\end{subarray}}\cdots.

Remark 7.12
.

Observe that in Definition
7.11
only the underlying
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module is reconstructed via descent, not the algebra structure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. This is due to the fact that the Amistur-C̆ech complex is only a complex of modules and not of algebras, in general.

Theorem 7.13
.

The structure pre-sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is a descendable pre-sheaf. Furthermore, under Assumption
7.3
the same holds on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
.

Proof.

Theorem
7.7
and Proposition
5.3
directly imply the claim for
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
. Conjecture
5.18
allows one to apply Theorem
7.7
to
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
, too.
∎

In all examples described so far it is possible to check that the structure pre-sheaf is, in fact, a sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
. This often happens for trivial reasons: in many examples the homotopical Zariski topology is the trivial topology on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(A)
, and thus all pre-sheaves are sheaves. We conjecture the following.

Conjecture 7.14
.

The structure pre-sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is a sheaf.

Proving Conjecture
7.14
amounts to applying Lurie-Barr-Beck to the adjunction

(7.2)

{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{A}\leftrightarrows\prod_{i\in I}{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{B_{i}}

for a cover
\{A\to B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for the homotopical Zariski topology. The main obstacle in doing so is that it requires a good understanding of the homological properties of the free algebras
A\{x\}={\mathbb{Z}}\langle x\rangle\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}A
introduced earlier — an understanding which we are lacking at the moment.

Remark 7.15
.

Let us make a final remark about descendable pre-sheaves.
A reason for introducing this notion is our analysis of the example of the quiver path algebra
kA_{2}
. For the latter, we have described the canonical morphism

\iota_{kA_{2}}:{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(kA_{2})\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2})

as the inclusion of the discrete topological space with three points as the three generic points of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(kA_{2})
. In this case the structure pre-sheaf is a sheaf, because the homotopical Zariski topology is trivial on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(kA_{2})
and hence all pre-sheaves on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(kA_{2})
are sheaves. Pulling back via
\iota_{kA_{2}}
just gives the same pre-sheaf on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(kA_{2})
(because
\iota_{kA_{2}}
is induced by the identity functor on
{\mathbf{Ouv}}(kA_{2})
). Then, Example
7.1
tells us that the resulting pre-sheaf is not a sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(kA_{2})
and its sheafification leads to a sheaf whose global sections is not
kA_{2}
. This latter feature is not desirable if we want to study the geometry of
kA_{2}
. But Example
7.2
shows that the structure pre-sheaf is a descendable pre-sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(kA_{2})
and thus it satisfies a local-to-global gluing process that is different from the one commonly used in sheaf theory. In view of the above, we would argue that sheaf theory is not adequate for studying the geometry of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(kA_{2})
, and an extension along the lines described in this section is required.

In this subsection we have briefly outlined what a noncommutative version of sheaf theory may look like. In the next subsection we will show that a suitable subcategory of the category of pre-ringed spaces is equivalent to the opposite category of
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, thus realising a weak form of Gelfand’s duality for noncommutative dga’s — and, in particular, for noncommutative rings.

7.2  Towards noncommutative Gelfand’s duality

In this section we provide a first version of a noncommutative extension of Gelfand’s duality to the category of rings. This extension is carried out in the algebraic setting of this paper; Gelfand duality for
C^{*}
-algebras will be discussed in a future work, where the machinery from
[
8
]
,
[
19
]
and related works will be employed. We will also elaborate on why the current version of our result is only a first step towards a more refined theory which will be carefully developed elsewhere.

Let us start by briefly recalling some key aspects of the duality between affine schemes and commutative rings. The algebraic version of Gelfand’s duality,
i.e.
the duality between affine schemes and commutative rings developed by Grothendieck, requires one to endow the topological spaces resulting from the construction of the spectrum with extra-structure. Indeed, this step is necessary to obtain a perfect duality. The extra-structure in question for
X={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
to be equipped with is the structure sheaf of rings
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
which allows one to discriminate between affine schemes that are indistinguishable as mere topological spaces. For example, any field extension
k\to l
induces a homeomorphism of the underlying spectra
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(l)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(k)
. Therefore, the setting of ringed spaces is what is needed for the theory of affine schemes. But it is still not enough to obtain a perfect duality, because if we consider two spectra of commutative rings
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
and
(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
regarded as ringed spaces, then there exist morphisms
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\to(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
that do not come from a morphism of commutative rings. For the benefit of the reader, we recall the following well known example of a morphism with this property.

Example 7.16
.

Let
X=({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Q}}),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Q}})})
and
Y=({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}}),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})})
be the classical affine schemes associated to
{\mathbb{Q}}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and consider the continuous map

f_{p}:\star={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Q}})\to\{p\}\subset{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})

sending the point of
X
to a closed point of
Y
identified by a prime number
p
. We have that

f_{p}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Z}})})=\mathop{\lim\limits_{\displaystyle\rightarrow}}_{U\supset\{p\}}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(U)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}.

Therefore, the canonical inclusion map
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}\to{\mathbb{Q}}
defines a morphism of ringed spaces, but this morphism does not come from a map of rings because the only morphism of rings
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}
sends
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}({\mathbb{Q}})
to the generic point.

The key observation here is that the map
{\mathbb{Z}}_{(p)}\to{\mathbb{Q}}
is not a local map of local rings, which naturally leads one to consider the category of locally ringed spaces. This latter is a non-full subcategory of the category of ringed spaces that realises a perfect duality with commutative rings.

Another way to rephrase the above observation is that it is possible to construct a functor

{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}:{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{Rings}}^{\rm op\,}={\mathbf{Aff}}\to{\mathbf{Top}}

given by
A\mapsto{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
which is not faithful, because it is not conservative: the functor
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}
loses some information about the category
{\mathbf{Aff}}
. Therefore, it must be enhanced to a functor

(7.3)

({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt},{\mathcal{O}}):{\mathbf{Aff}}\to\mathbf{RingSp}

to the category of ringed spaces. This latter is faithful, so does not lose information, but it is not fully faithful.

At this point, the task is to describe the essential image of this functor. This has led to the introduction of the category of locally ringed spaces for which Grothendieck proved that the functor

(7.4)

({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt},{\mathcal{O}}):{\mathbf{Aff}}\to\mathbf{LocRingSp}

is fully faithful, so that the latter duality is
a duality,
i.e.
a fully faithful inclusion.
In this work we do not achieve the goal of describing a perfect duality between the category of rings and a suitable category of (homotopically) ringed spaces, like in the case of (
7.4
). What we can achieve is the less ambitious task of constructing a faithful functor like in the case of (
7.3
), leaving the task of describing the essential image of the functor we construct for future work.

Let us consider a variation of the definition of ringed space. The issue we faced at the beginning of this section is that in the noncommutative case we do not know whether the association
U\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
is a (homotopical) sheaf (although we have Conjecture
7.14
in this direction), but only a pre-sheaf (see Corollary
7.10
).

Therefore, we can generalise the notion of ringed space to that of pre-ringed space.

Definition 7.17
.

A
pre-ringed space
(or
site
) is a topological space (or site)
X
equipped with a structure pre-sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}:{\mathbf{Ouv}}(X)^{\rm op\,}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. A morphism of a pre-ringed spaces
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\to(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
is a pair given by a continuous map
f:X\to Y
and morphism of pre-sheaves
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\to f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. We denote the category of pre-ringed spaces (resp. sites) by
\mathbf{PreRingSp}
(resp.
\mathbf{PreRingSites}
).

The construction given above defines a ringed site
({\mathbf{Zar}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
for any
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. In the next remark we explain how to extend this construction to the topological space
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
.

Remark 7.18
.

Given an open subset
U\subset{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
, one can always write
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
where
U_{i}={\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B_{i})
are open subsets associated to homotopical epimorphisms
A\to B_{i}
. Then we define

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)}(U)={\mathbb{R}}\lim_{i\in I}N^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(U_{i}))\cong{\mathbb{R}}\lim_{i\in I}N^{\bullet}(B_{i})\,,

where
N^{\bullet}(B_{i})
is the Amistur–C̆ech complex introduced earlier when discussing the comonadic descent. Since for any affine cover
\bigcup_{j\in J}V_{j}=U_{i}
we have that

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)}(U_{i})={\mathbb{R}}\lim_{j\in J}N^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(V_{i}))\,,

i.e.
the value of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)}(U_{i})
does not depend on the cover used to compute it (because by Theorem
7.13
the structure pre-sheaf is descendable), a standard computation shows that also the value of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)}(U)
is independent of the chosen cover. The problem with this procedure is that the resulting pre-sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is only a pre-sheaf of
A
-modules, so we cannot endow sections with the structure of a ring.
Assuming the validity of Conjecture
7.14
we can also extend
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to a sheaf of homotopical rings on all
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
using the comonadicity of the adjunction (
7.2
). We keep using the pre-ringed site
({\mathbf{Zar}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
, although conjecturally its use can be replaced by the pre-ringed space
({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
.

Observe that, unlike in the classical notion of ringed space (or site), in Definition
7.17
we allow the values of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to be homotopical rings; if these are concentrated in degree
0
we obtain a classical pre-sheaf of rings. The important feature brought about by Definition
7.17
, when put in the context of the discussion of duality made thus far, is expressed by the following lemma.

Lemma 7.19
.

The category of ringed spaces (resp. sites) is a full subcategory of the category of pre-ringed spaces (resp. sites)
26
26

26

We prove the lemma only for classical ringed spaces and pre-ringed spaces, but the same result holds for the category of homotopically ringed spaces replacing hom-sets with mapping spaces. We refrain to enter in the technical details of the homotopical version of the lemma because the best way to state and prove it is using the language of
\infty
-categories. We also emphasise that in the noncommutative situation the homotopical version of the lemma is the more relevant one.
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma for ringed spaces; the same proof works for ringed sites as well.
Clearly, every ringed space is a pre-ringed space in a canonical way. We only need to check that the notions of morphism in the two categories agree. Let us consider two ringed spaces
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\to(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
. Then, given a morphism of topological spaces
f:X\to Y
, the subset of

{\rm Hom\,}_{\mathbf{RingSp}}((X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}),(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}))

corresponding to
f
is given by the set

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sh}}(Y)}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y},f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}))\,.

But since the direct image of a sheaf is always a sheaf and the category of sheaves is a full subcategory of the category of pre-sheaves, we get a canonical bijection

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Sh}}(Y)}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y},f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}))\cong{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{Psh}}(Y)}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y},f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}))\,.

But the latter is the subset of

{\rm Hom\,}_{\mathbf{PreRingSp}}((X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}),(Y,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}))

corresponding to
f
, and we have the claim.
∎

In view of Lemma
7.19
, the
classical duality issue of scheme theory of describing the essential image of the functor

A\mapsto({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)})

can be formulated in an equivalent way in
\mathbf{RingSp}
or
\mathbf{PreRingSp}
. The advantage is that the latter formulation makes sense also in the noncommutative case, where we do not know if the structure pre-sheaf is a sheaf. What remains to be checked in the noncommutative setting is that the resulting functor is faithful,
i.e.
no information about the original category is lost. Our current version of Gelfand duality for dg-algebras precisely proves this claim.

Theorem 7.20
.

The (contravariant) functor

(7.5)

A\mapsto({\mathbf{Zar}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{A})

from the category
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
to the category
\mathbf{PreRingSites}
is faithful.

Proof.

Let
A,B\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. We need to check that the map

{\rm Hom\,}_{{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(A,B)\to{\rm Hom\,}_{\mathbf{PreRingSites}}(({\mathbf{Zar}}_{B},{\mathcal{O}}_{B}),({\mathbf{Zar}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{A}))

is injective. Let
f,g:A\to B
be two different maps. We have defined the maps of topological spaces
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}:{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(B)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
induced by
f
and
g
in Corollary
4.15
, as the maps induced by the pseudofunctor of Proposition
4.13
. It is immediate, by definition, that in both cases we have

f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{B})(X_{A})=B,\qquad g_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{B})(X_{A})=B,

where
X_{A}
is the top element of the lattice of
{\mathbf{Zar}}_{A}
.
Once again by definition, we have that the morphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{A}(X_{A})=A\to f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{B})(X_{A})=B

is
f
, and the morphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{A}(X_{A})=A\to g_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{B})(X_{A})=B

is
g
. Thus the two morphisms of pre-ringed sites are different.
∎

Theorem
7.20
has an immediate consequence, which can be stated in classical terms.

Corollary 7.21
.

The category of rings is equivalent to a subcategory of the category
\mathbf{PreRingSites}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is fully faithful, therefore the composition with the Gelfand duality of Theorem
7.20
is a faithful functor.
∎

Remark 7.22
.

Let us observe that, although the statement of Corollary
7.21
is of classical nature, to make sense of it it is not enough to remain in the framework of rings but it is necessary to use the (homotopy) category of dg-algebras, because the natural structural pre-sheaf on
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is a homotopical pre-sheaf of dg-algebras not necessarily concentrated in degree
0
(if
A
is not commutative) — see,
e.g.
, Example
4.8
.

Theorem
7.20
can be rightfully viewed as the noncommutative analogue of Gelfand’s duality, in that it describes
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm op\,}
(and hence its full subcategory
{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\rm op\,}
) as a (non-full) subcategory of
\mathbf{PreRingSites}
. This is formally the same as the duality between commutative rings and affine schemes described as a (non-full) subcategory of
\mathbf{PreRingSp}
. In order for this to be useful in doing geometry, one should also have a description of the essential image of the functor (
7.5
). This consists of a very specific family of pre-ringed sites, because we have shown that modules on
A
(and hence the associated pre-sheaf of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
-modules they determine) satisfy comonadic descent,
i.e.
they have a pre-sheafy local-to-global behaviour. We postpone this study in a future work.

We conclude this subsection with an improvement on the description of the essential image of the functor (
7.5
). This is achieved by using the following variation of the notion of ringed space.

Definition 7.23
.

A pre-ringed space
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
(resp. site) is called
descendable
if the structure pre-sheaf is a descendable pre-sheaf in the sense of Definition
7.11
. We denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{PreRingSp}
(resp. sites) of descendable pre-ringed spaces (resp. sites) by
\mathbf{DescPreRingSp}
(resp.
\mathbf{DescPreRingSites}
).

Theorem 7.24
.

The essential image of the Gelfand duality functor (
7.5
) lies in the subcategory
\mathbf{DescPreRingSites}
.

Proof.

Theorem
7.13
immediately implies that
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
is always a descendable pre-sheaf.
∎

Remark 7.25
.

Under Conjecture
7.14
the statement of Theorem
7.24
can be upgraded to a faithful embedding into the category
\mathbf{PreRingSp}
.

The notion of descendable pre-sheaf is a possible noncommutative generalisation of the notion of quasi-coherent sheaf, as the upcoming definition and proposition indicate.

Definition 7.26
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
be a descendable pre-ringed space (resp. site). A pre-sheaf of right (or left)
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules
{\mathscr{F}}
is said to be
descendable
if for all covers
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
we have

{\mathscr{F}}(U)\cong{\mathbb{R}}\lim_{i\in I}\left(\prod_{i\in I}{\mathscr{F}}({U_{i}})\rightrightarrows\prod_{i,j\in I}{\mathscr{F}}({U_{i}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U})}^{\mathbb{L}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}({U_{j}})\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\end{subarray}}\cdots\right).

We denote the full subcategory of the category of pre-sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules consisting of descendable pre-sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules by
\mathbf{Desc}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-
{\mathbf{Mod}}
.

Proposition 7.27
.

Consider the site
({\mathbf{Zar}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
of an object
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. There is an equivalence of categories
\mathbf{Desc}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-
{\mathbf{Mod}}\cong{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Mod}}_{A}
.

Proof.

Proposition
7.7
immediately implies the claim.
∎

7.3  Concluding remarks

We would like to make some further remarks on the constructions described in this last section. So far we have been focussing on understanding the noncommutative aspects of our definitions, but they apply as well to commutative rings. It is clear that by definition our notions are compatible with the classical notions of algebraic geometry, because they have been designed to have this property. Nevertheless, we would like to make such observations more explicit.

Proposition 7.28
.

Let
A\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be commutative. The pre-ringed space
({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
is a (homotopically) ringed space.

Proof.

Theorem
5.20
implies that the Amistur-C̆ech complex of comonadic descent considered in Definition
7.23
agrees with the sheaf-theoretic C̆ech complex of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
. Therefore, in this case being a descendable pre-sheaf is equivalent to being a (homotopical) sheaf
27
27

27

As already underlined, when
A
is commutative all its localizations are commutative as well and concentrated in degree
0
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)}(U)
is a discrete commutative simplicial ring for all open subsets
U\subset{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
and hence the condition of homotopical sheafyness reduces to usual sheafyness.
.
∎

Remark 7.29
.

In many situations where we have that
A
is discrete,
i.e.
concentrated in degree
0
, and all its localizations are discrete as well (for example in the case of commutative Noetherian rings, see Example
4.8
) Proposition
7.28
implies that the space
({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
is an actual ringed space, not a homotopical one.

When
A
is a discrete commutative ring, the structure of pre-sheaf of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)
is compatible with that of
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
.

Proposition 7.30
.

Let
A
be a discrete commutative ring.
The canonical map of topological spaces

\pi_{A}:{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}_{\rm fine\,}(A)\to{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)

obtained in Proposition
5.10
induces a canonical morphism of pre-ringed spaces

\pi_{A}:({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{A})\to({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{A})

that is a map of ringed spaces in the case when
A
is Noetherian.

Proof.

We remark that since
{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
is a spectral space, it is enough to specify the values of a sheaf on a base of compact open subsets. Therefore, for any affine open subset
U\subset{\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.28036pt\tiny{\mathtt{G}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A)
we have that the map
\pi_{A}
induces the identity morphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{A}(U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\pi_{A})_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{A})(U)={\mathcal{O}}_{A}(\pi_{A}^{-1}(U))

that is obviously a morphism of pre-sheaves. The claim in the Noetherian case is a consequence of Proposition
7.28
.
∎

We can finally also sketch how a full theory of derived noncommutative schemes based on the results above looks like. The notion of descendable pre-ringed space permits to give a reasonable definition of derived noncommutative scheme.

Definition 7.31
.

A
derived noncommutative scheme
is a descendable pre-ringed site
(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
such that there is an open cover of open sub-pre-ringed sites
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
such that
(U_{i},{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}})\cong({\mathbf{Zar}}_{A_{i}},{\mathcal{O}}_{A_{i}})
for some
A_{i}\in{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{Rings}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Remark 7.32
.

Under Conjecture
7.14
, Defnition
7.31
can be upgraded using the ringed spaces
({\mathrm{Spec}^{\hskip-1.13809pt\tiny{\mathtt{NC}}}\hskip-1.42262pt}(A),{\mathcal{O}}_{A})
associated with affine noncommutative schemes.

We emphasise that Definition
7.31
possibly gives a good notion of noncommutative scheme, but without a better understanding of the essential image of the functor (
7.5
) it is not clear what the correct notion of morphism between such spaces should be. Therefore, the first step to proceed in the study of derived noncommutative scheme theory is to complete the study of the noncommutative Gelfand duality obtained above.
